					<description><![CDATA[<p>HBM shortages threaten AI expansion; TSMC weighs price hikes; advanced packaging ramps; domain-specialized AI models; Infineon and UMC boost fab investments; Intel's quantum move; Lam and imec advance 3D integration; Google expands TPUs; AI-driven earnings; CPU benchmarking methodology for agentic AI.</p>
<p>The post <a href="https://semiengineering.com/chip-industry-week-in-review-162/">Chip Industry Week In Review</a> appeared first on <a href="https://semiengineering.com">Semiconductor Engineering</a>.</p>
]]></description>
										<content:encoded><![CDATA[<h3 class="p1"><strong>AI memory and capacity</strong></h3>
<ul>
<li><strong>SK hynix</strong> expects <a href="https://news.skhynix.com/en/q3-2026-business-results/">memory supply constraints to persist through 2030</a>, with DRAM and HBM volumes effectively sold out through 2029. <strong>Samsung</strong> expects <a href="https://www.mt.co.kr/en/industry/2026/10/29/2026102910520568042?">tight memory supply through 2028</a>, citing lengthy fab construction and equipment lead times, larger dies, and greater process complexity in next-gen HBM, as well as a shift of DRAM capacity toward HBM. Samsung said its 2027 HBM supply is already sold out.</li>
<li><strong>Solidigm</strong>, SK hynix&#8217;s U.S. subsidiary, reportedly is discussing a proposed $45B <a href="https://news.bloomberglaw.com/capital-markets/solidigm-dangles-new-45-billion-us-plant-to-upgrade-china-site">NAND flash</a> fab with the U.S. Commerce Department.</li>
<li class="p3">China’s <strong>CXMT</strong> plans to soon begin limited <a href="https://www.reuters.com/world/china/chinas-cxmt-start-advanced-ai-memory-production-despite-poor-yields-sources-say-2026-10-28/">production of HBM3E memory</a>, despite manufacturing yields of just 20% to 30%, per Reuters.</li>
<li><strong>Anthropic</strong> warned in its IPO filing that <a href="https://www.sec.gov/Archives/edgar/data/2130357/000162828026067168/anthropicpbc-sx1.htm"><span class="s1">HBM shortages and limited TSMC manufacturing capacity</span></a> could constrain AI expansion.</li>
</ul>
<h3 class="p1"><strong>Advanced packaging </strong></h3>
<ul>
<li><strong>ASE</strong> forecasts a <a href="https://media-aseholdco.todayir.com/20261029160705761664454_en.pdf">doubling of its advanced-packaging</a> revenue in 2027, and <strong>Amkor</strong> is ramping new <a href="https://ir.amkor.com/static-files/70fef79b-6e7c-4f65-91d1-2ee19e575e3c">high-density fan-out and 2.5D</a> packaging programs.</li>
<li><strong>TSMC</strong> reportedly is considering a 20% <a href="https://www.trendforce.com/news/2026/10/27/news-tsmc-reportedly-eyes-20-cowos-price-hike-skips-equipment-price-renegotiations-amid-ai-demand-surge/">price increase for CoWoS</a> as AI demand continues to strain advanced-packaging capacity.</li>
<li><strong>Applied Materials</strong> plans to <a href="https://www.appliedmaterials.com/us/en/newsroom/press-releases/102626-increase-investment-besi-semiconductor.html">increase its stake</a> in <strong>Besi</strong> to ~20%, deepening a strategic partnership that is expanding beyond hybrid bonding into new interconnect architectures and advanced packaging technologies for AI.</li>
</ul>
<h3 class="p1"><strong>Manufacturing </strong></h3>
<ul>
<li><strong>Intel Foundry</strong> reported improving <a href="https://stockanalysis.com/stocks/intc/transcripts/714242-q3-2026/">manufacturing execution</a> in Q3, citing better yields and cycle times on 18A. The company also released its 14A 0.9 PDK in October and said it continues to engage prospective external customers around 18A-P and 14A, while advancing its EMIB-T advanced-packaging technology.</li>
<li><strong>Infineon</strong> plans to invest €1.1B through 2034 at its Villach, Austria facility to establish Europe&#8217;s first high-volume manufacturing facility for <a href="https://www.infineon.com/press-release/2026/infpr202610-021">next-gen GaN power</a> semiconductors on 300mm wafers.</li>
<li><b>UMC</b> approved an additional <a href="https://www.umc.com/upload/media/08_Investors/Financials/Quarterly_Results/Quarterly_2020-2029_English_pdf/2026/Q3_2026/UMC26Q3_report.pdf"><span class="s1">$3.2B in capital spending</span></a> to accelerate its capacity expansion in Singapore and Tainan as demand grows for advanced packaging and silicon photonics.</li>
<li><strong>Lam Research</strong> and <strong>imec</strong> expanded their collaboration on advanced <a href="https://newsroom.lamresearch.com/lam-imec-3d-integration-dry-resist-ai-semiconductor-scaling">3D integration, next-gen memory, and high-NA EUV</a> patterning. The work includes deposition, etch, and plasma dicing processes for advanced packaging, along with Lam&#8217;s Aether dry resist technology.</li>
</ul>
<h3><strong>AI tools and accelerators</strong></h3>
<ul>
<li><strong>Cadence</strong> introduced a portfolio of domain-specialized <a href="https://newsroom.cadence.com/press-releases/press-release-details/2026/Cadence-Introduces-Maestro-a-Domain-Specialized-AI-Model-Portfolio-to-Revolutionize-Agentic-Chip-and-System-Design/default.aspx">AI models</a> for agentic chip and system design. Integrated with Cadence&#8217;s EDA tools, the portfolio spans multiple model sizes and architectures, combining post-trained LLMs, decision models, and self-improving agent skills.</li>
<li><strong>Arm</strong> introduced a new <a href="https://newsroom.arm.com/news/arm-agi-cpu-agentic-benchmarks">CPU benchmarking methodology for agentic AI</a>. The framework evaluates CPU performance across agentic workloads, including tool execution, orchestration, and reasoning, using repeatable measurements and end-to-end task outcomes.</li>
<li><strong>Google</strong> is expanding the reach of its custom AI accelerators, with <strong>OpenAI</strong> reportedly planning to <a href="https://www.theinformation.com/articles/openai-longtime-nvidia-customer-plans-buy-googles-ai-chips">purchase its TPUs</a>, reports The Information. Separately, <strong>Oracle</strong> will offer Google’s TPUs through its cloud infrastructure beginning in 2027 and plans to add <strong>Arm</strong>’s <a href="https://newsroom.arm.com/news/arm-agi-cpu-oracle-cloud-infrastructure">AGI CPUs</a> in fall 2027.</li>
<li><strong>Broadcom</strong>&#8216;s XPUs and networking equipment will support <strong>Anthropic</strong>&#8216;s AI infrastructure expansion, beginning in 2027, under a <a href="https://investors.broadcom.com/news-releases/news-release-details/bank-america-blackstone-citi-and-morgan-stanley-announce">$63B financing</a> arrangement.</li>
<li><strong>Meta</strong> is expanding deployments of <a href="https://nvidianews.nvidia.com/news/nvidia-and-meta-to-expand-collaboration-on-muse-and-muse-family-of-models-amid-robust-growth">Nvidia</a> and <a href="https://newsroom.amd.com/news/amd-meta-advance-strategic-partnership-power-metas-ai-infrastructure/">AMD</a> AI infrastructure across its data centers, with both chipmakers also taking stakes in the company.</li>
</ul>
<h3><strong>Deals and funding</strong></h3>
<ul>
<li><strong>Intel</strong> plans to <a href="https://www.intc.com/news-events/press-releases/detail/1784/intel-to-acquire-classiq-to-create-a-full-stack-quantum">acquire</a> <strong>Classiq</strong>, a developer of automated quantum algorithm synthesis software. Intel says the acquisition will give it a full stack of quantum offerings, adding to its silicon spin-qubit technology and manufacturing.</li>
<li><strong>FuriosaAI</strong> raised $620M to scale production of its <a href="https://furiosa.ai/blog/furiosaai-620-million-series-d">AI inference accelerator</a>.</li>
<li><strong>Unconventional AI </strong>plans to <a href="https://unconv.ai/blog/unconventional-ai-to-acquire-neureality/">acquire</a> <strong>NeuReality</strong>.</li>
<li><strong>Unconfirmed</strong>: <strong>Marvell</strong> is reportedly in advanced talks to <a href="https://www.bloomberg.com/news/articles/2026-10-28/marvell-is-said-to-near-deal-for-lumentum-in-ai-related-megadeal">acquire optical components maker</a> <strong>Lumentum</strong>, per Bloomberg. <strong>Onsemi</strong> reportedly is in <a href="https://www.theinformation.com/articles/chipmaker-onsemi-talks-buy-startup-1-billion">talks to acquire</a> <strong>Ferric</strong>, a startup developing integrated voltage regulators for AI processors, for nearly $1B, according to The Information.</li>
</ul>
<h3 id="Earnings"><strong>Earnings</strong></h3>
<p><strong>Quarterly revenue</strong> and % growth versus the <strong>same quarter in the prior year</strong>, unless otherwise indicated:</p>
<ul>
<li><strong>Advantest</strong>: ¥477.7B, <a href="https://www.advantest.com/document/en/investors/ir-library/result/JE_BIZ_261028_slide.pdf">up 82%</a>, with robust tester demand for AI.</li>
<li><strong>Amkor</strong>: Net income $191M, <a href="https://ir.amkor.com/static-files/0ef4c21c-9b2a-4c22-be53-4fd891d17aa0">up 50%</a>, due to a richer mix of <a href="https://ir.amkor.com/static-files/70fef79b-6e7c-4f65-91d1-2ee19e575e3c">high-value advanced packaging</a>.</li>
<li><strong>ASE: </strong>NT$244.2B, <a href="https://media-aseholdco.todayir.com/20261029160705761664454_en.pdf">up 45%</a>.</li>
<li><strong>Cadence</strong>: $1.6B, <a href="https://newsroom.cadence.com/press-releases/press-release-details/2026/Cadence-Reports-Third-Quarter-2026-Financial-Results/default.aspx">up 22%</a>, with a record backlog of $8.2B.</li>
<li><strong>Intel</strong>: $16.5B, <a href="https://www.intc.com/news-events/press-releases/detail/1786/intel-reports-third-quarter-2026-financial-results">up 21%</a>, with 78% data center and AI sales and foundry revenue up 58%.</li>
<li><strong>Rambus:</strong> Record $218M, <a href="https://investor.rambus.com/press-releases/press-release-details/2026/Rambus-Reports-Third-Quarter-2026-Financial-Results/default.aspx">up 22%</a>.</li>
<li><strong>Samsung</strong>: KRW 195.3T, <a href="https://news.samsung.com/global/samsung-electronics-announces-third-quarter-2026-results">up 127%</a>, major ramp in HBM4.</li>
<li><strong>SK hynix</strong>: Record KRW 104.6T, <a href="https://news.skhynix.com/en/q3-2026-business-results/">up 328%</a>.</li>
<li><strong>Teradyne:</strong> $1.3B, <a href="https://investors.teradyne.com/news-events/press-releases/detail/455/teradyne-reports-third-quarter-2026-results">up 70%</a>, with record memory and systems test revenue.</li>
<li><strong>UMC</strong>: NT$74.2B, <a href="https://www.umc.com/upload/media/08_Investors/Financials/Quarterly_Results/Quarterly_2020-2029_English_pdf/2026/Q3_2026/UMC26Q3_report.pdf">up ~25.6%</a>, with fab utilization reaching 92%.</li>
</ul>

<h2><a href="https://www.enpro-ast.com/"><img data-recalc-dims="1" fetchpriority="high" decoding="async" class="alignnone wp-image-24283368" src="https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/10/Oct27-EnPro_AST-1270x240.jpg.jpeg?resize=947%2C180&#038;ssl=1" alt="" width="947" height="180" srcset="https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/10/Oct27-EnPro_AST-1270x240.jpg.jpeg?resize=300%2C57&amp;ssl=1 300w, https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/10/Oct27-EnPro_AST-1270x240.jpg.jpeg?resize=1024%2C194&amp;ssl=1 1024w, https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/10/Oct27-EnPro_AST-1270x240.jpg.jpeg?resize=768%2C145&amp;ssl=1 768w, https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/10/Oct27-EnPro_AST-1270x240.jpg.jpeg?resize=600%2C113&amp;ssl=1 600w, https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/10/Oct27-EnPro_AST-1270x240.jpg.jpeg?w=1270&amp;ssl=1 1270w" sizes="(max-width: 947px) 100vw, 947px" /></a></h2>
<hr />
<h2 id="Global"><strong>Global</strong></h2>
<h3><strong>Asia</strong></h3>
<ul>
<li><strong>Vietnam</strong>&#8216;s first semiconductor fab <a href="https://www.reuters.com/world/asia-pacific/vietnams-first-chip-plant-unlikely-meet-2027-target-sources-say-no-deal-yet-with-2026-10-26/">may miss its 2027 trial-production target</a>, according to Reuters. The estimated US$1.3B Viettel project reportedly has yet to begin construction, while negotiations for ASML lithography equipment remain unresolved. Viettel maintains the project is on schedule.</li>
<li>South Korea&#8217;s <strong>PSK</strong> is planning a new US$125M semiconductor <a href="https://www.thelec.kr/news/articleView.html">equipment manufacturing facility in Yongin&#8217;s</a> semiconductor cluster.</li>
</ul>
<h3><strong>Americas</strong></h3>
<ul>
<li><strong>ITIF</strong> says the U.S.-led <a href="https://itif.org/publications/2026/10/27/pax-silica-timeline/">Pax Silica initiative</a> has grown to 26 countries, coordinating on semiconductors, critical minerals, energy, advanced manufacturing, and AI infrastructure to reduce dependence on vulnerable supply chains.</li>
<li><strong>Imec</strong> and <strong>Indiana University</strong> formed a <a href="https://www.imec-int.com/en/press/iu-lands-partnership-international-semiconductor-powerhouse-imec">semiconductor research partnership</a> focused on advanced electronics, photonics, and workforce development. Initial research will test imec-developed electronic and photonic components in extreme environments, including space.</li>
<li><strong>Cadence</strong> will <a href="https://www.cadence.com/en_US/home/company/newsroom/press-releases/pr/2026/cadence-joins-the-genesis-mission-consortium-to-advance-ai.html">provide</a> U.S. <strong>Department of Energy</strong> National Laboratories with access to EDA, SDA, multiphysics simulation, digital twins, advanced packaging, and 3D-IC workflows, along with technical expertise.</li>
</ul>
<h3><strong>Europe</strong></h3>
<ul>
<li><strong>SUSS MicroTec</strong> will <a href="https://www.suss.com/ko/news/corporate-news/2026/suss-optimizes-global-production-network-to-support-continued-growth-and-updates-guidance-for-fiscal-year-2026">consolidate semiconductor equipment production</a> in Zhubei, Taiwan, and Sternenfels, Germany, and end manufacturing at its Garching facility near Munich.</li>
</ul>
<div style="background: #f7f7f7; border: 1.5px solid #888888; padding: 16px 20px; margin: 24px 0; border-radius: 3px;">
<h2 id="In-Depth"><strong>In-Depth</strong></h2>
<p><strong>Semiconductor Engineering</strong> published the <a href="https://semiengineering.com/newsletter/systems-design-oct-2026/">Systems and Design newsletter</a>, including:</p>
<ul>
<li><strong>Special Report</strong>: <a href="https://semiengineering.com/ai-raises-the-bar-for-tomorrows-engineers/">AI Raises The Bar For Tomorrow’s Engineers</a></li>
<li><a href="https://semiengineering.com/auto-generation-of-properties-methodology-insertion/">Auto-Generation Of Properties: Methodology Insertion</a></li>
<li><a href="https://semiengineering.com/rethinking-shift-left-in-chip-design/">Rethinking Shift Left In Chip Design</a></li>
</ul>
<p><strong>Plus</strong>:</p>
<ul>
<li><strong>The imec Report</strong>: <a href="https://semiengineering.com/the-imec-report-scaling-optical-interconnects-for-the-ai-era/">Scaling Optical Interconnects For The AI Era</a></li>
<li><a href="https://semiengineering.com/bringing-down-test-costs-for-co-packaged-optics/">Bringing Down Test Costs For Co-Packaged Optics</a></li>
<li><a href="https://semiengineering.com/how-much-can-semiconductor-models-hide/">How Much Can Semiconductor Models Hide</a>?</li>
</ul>
</div>
<hr />
<h2 id="New Technologies"><strong>New Technologies</strong></h2>
<h3><strong>Chip design and verification</strong></h3>
<ul>
<li><strong>ChipAgents</strong> is expanding its <a href="https://chipagents.ai/newsroom/chipagents-ic-star-full-stack-agentic-ai">agentic AI</a> platform across digital and analog chip design with a neural engine that combines persistent design knowledge, advanced reasoning and orchestration, dynamic model routing, and physics-aware models. It will also work with <strong>Nvidia</strong> to fine-tune Nemotron models.</li>
<li><strong>Axiomise</strong> and <strong>Agnisys</strong> teamed up to verify <a href="https://www.agnisys.com/news/agnisys-and-axiomise-collaborate-to-accelerate-noc-design-and-formal-sign-off/">NoCs</a> using formal technologies.</li>
</ul>
<h3><strong>AI power delivery</strong></h3>
<ul>
<li data-pm-slice="1 1 [&quot;list&quot;,{&quot;spread&quot;:false,&quot;start&quot;:1793,&quot;end&quot;:2245},&quot;regular_list_item&quot;,{&quot;start&quot;:1793,&quot;end&quot;:2245}]"><strong>Infineon</strong> introduced new AI power-delivery technologies, including a <a href="https://www.infineon.com/technology-news/2026/inftn202610-015">±400 V/800 V</a> rack power-distribution reference board, a <a href="https://www.infineon.com/technology-news/2026/inftn202610-016">5.6 kW M-CRPS </a>reference design, and 900 V monolithic bidirectional <a href="https://www.infineon.com/technology-news/2026/inftn202610-014">GaN switches</a> for solid-state transformers in AI data centers.</li>
</ul>
<h3><strong>Compute, memory and photonics</strong></h3>
<ul>
<li><strong>PsiQuantum</strong> demonstrated an 8×8 <a href="https://www.psiquantum.com/news-import/psiquantum-unveils-optical-circuit-switch-technology-built-on-advanced-low-loss-silicon-photonics-platform">optical circuit switch</a> built on silicon photonics for AI infrastructure.</li>
<li><strong>TetraMem</strong> taped out a 12nm analog in-memory computing SoC based on multi-level <a href="https://www.businesswire.com/news/home/20261027687318/en/TetraMem-Advances-Analog-In-Memory-Computing-with-Tapeout-of-New-12nm-AI-SoC">RRAM</a> and developed using <strong>Synopsys</strong>&#8216; cloud EDA tools.</li>
<li><strong>Fraunhofer IIS</strong> integrated permanent micromagnets directly into the silicon substrate of a <a href="https://www.iis.fraunhofer.de/en/pr/2026/press-release-microbias-integrated-micromagnets.html">magnetic sensor chip</a> at wafer level.</li>
</ul>
<h3><strong>Automotive</strong></h3>
<ul>
<li><strong>Infineon</strong> introduced a <a href="https://www.infineon.com/technology-news/2026/inftn202610-019">chip-embedding</a> platform for high-voltage power electronics, <a href="https://www.infineon.com/technology-news/2026/inftn202610-018">LinearFET</a> technology for solid-state power distribution in EVs and SDVs, new <a href="https://www.infineon.com/technology-news/2026/inftn202610-020">MOSFETs</a> for 12 V and 48 V vehicle systems, and a <a href="https://www.infineon.com/technology-news/2026/inftn202610-017">high-voltage BMS</a> reference design.</li>
<li><strong>STMicro </strong>released an <a href="https://newsroom.st.com/media-center/press-item.html/stmicroelectronics-introduces-first-automotive-safety-combination-sensors-for-mass-market-applications-p4796-2.html">automotive safety sensor</a> platform that combines accelerometers and gyroscopes for ADAS and vehicle-safety applications.</li>
<li><strong>Bosch</strong> debuted a new <a href="https://www.bosch-semiconductors.com/stories-and-events/smi340-combines-vehicle-safety-and-adas/">MEMS inertial sensor</a> platform, combining ADAS and vehicle-safety functions up to ASIL D.</li>
</ul>
<hr />
<h2 id="Research"><strong>Research</strong></h2>
<p data-pm-slice="1 1 []"><strong>TU Munich</strong> researchers developed <a href="https://semiengineering.com/neural-networks-speed-3nm-nanosheet-spice-simulation-tu-munich/">neural-network-based transistor models</a> for 3nm nanosheet devices, accelerating standard-cell library characterization by up to 8× compared with a conventional compact model. The models kept delay-estimation errors below 0.11%.</p>
<p>A <strong>Brown University</strong>-led team demonstrated <a href="https://www.brown.edu/news/2026-10-28/multiferroic-dead-zone">ultrathin multiferroic films</a> that retain ferroelectric and magnetic properties at thicknesses of just a few angstroms, overcoming the dead-layer effect in bismuth ferrite. The films also exhibited altermagnetic properties, potentially enabling low-power memory and logic devices.</p>
<p><strong>Chinese Academy of Sciences</strong>-led researchers developed a <a href="https://www.nature.com/articles/s41563-026-02780-7">III-nitride-based in-sensor computing device</a> that uses interfacial polarization to separate photogenerated carriers and reduce recombination losses. The sensor-memristor achieved responsivity on the order of 10⁶ A/W, an on/off ratio of 10⁵, and maintained high performance at temperatures up to 300°C.</p>
<p><strong>University of Oxford</strong>-led researchers identified a <a href="https://www.ox.ac.uk/news/2026-10-28-semiconductor-breakthrough-overcomes-major-challenge-in-capturing-solar-energy">pseudo-direct semiconductor in bismuth sulfide</a> that combines strong light absorption with slower electron–hole recombination. Its unusual electronic structure was estimated to reduce recombination rates by about 75% at room temperature compared with conventional semiconductors with similarly strong absorption, potentially improving solar-cell efficiency.</p>
<hr />
<h2 id="Security"><strong>Security</strong></h2>
<p data-pm-slice="1 1 []"><strong>MITRE</strong>’s CWE Program released <a href="https://cwe.mitre.org/data/index.html">CWE 5.0</a>, adding new weaknesses, including <a href="https://cwe.mitre.org/data/definitions/1461.html">Hardware Simulation/Synthesis Mismatch</a>. The new hardware weakness covers security-relevant cases where RTL behaves differently after synthesis, potentially exposing data, altering protected state, or bypassing security controls in the resulting hardware.</p>
<h3><strong>Alerts and hacks</strong></h3>
<ul>
<li><strong>TianoCore</strong> disclosed a critical vulnerability in its widely used EDK II UEFI firmware. The <a href="https://github.com/tianocore/edk2/security/advisories/GHSA-99cj-7hg9-mp4m">memory-corruption flaw</a> could allow an unauthenticated network attacker to execute code during PXE boot, before the OS loads and before Secure Boot or measured boot can verify any OS component.</li>
<li><strong>Supermicro</strong> disclosed nine <a href="https://www.supermicro.com/en/support/security_BMC_IPMI_Oct_2026_1">BMC firmware vulnerabilities</a> across two Oct. 26 advisories, including a critical flaw that could let an unauthenticated network attacker execute arbitrary IPMI commands on an active session. The disclosures also cover privilege-escalation, key-exposure, memory-access, and <a href="https://www.supermicro.com/fr-fr/support/security_BMC_IPMI_Oct_2026_2">command-injection</a> flaws.</li>
<li><strong>Keystone</strong> said a breach exposed a key used to <a href="https://teapawt.org/KeystoneWallet/article/2114719318722404578">authenticate its hardware wallets</a>, potentially allowing counterfeit devices to pass authenticity checks. The company is destroying 18,835 unsold devices containing the compromised key.</li>
</ul>
<h3><strong>Government security news</strong></h3>
<ul>
<li><strong>NIST</strong> finalized its <a href="https://csrc.nist.gov/pubs/ir/8547/final">road map for transitioning to PQC</a>, identifying the quantum-vulnerable standards that federal systems and technology providers will need to replace with quantum-resistant algorithms.</li>
<li>NIST also explained its <a href="https://www.nist.gov/blogs/taking-measure/measurement-research-could-help-devices-tell-you-if-theyve-been-tampered">RF-fingerprinting technique for detecting hardware tampering</a>, using subtle differences in devices’ radio emissions to identify altered or substituted components with more than 95% accuracy in testing.</li>
</ul>
<h3><strong>New capabilities and tools</strong></h3>
<ul>
<li><strong>Eclypsium</strong> and <strong>Nvidia</strong> demonstrated how a <a href="https://eclypsium.com/press-release/eclypsium-and-nvidia-collaborate-to-address-emerging-ai-infrastructure-attack-surface-and-strengthen-ai-trust/">compromised privileged component in AI compute</a> infrastructure could alter model weights and outputs during live inference without modifying the stored model.</li>
<li><strong>Altera</strong> introduced its Agilex 2X FPGA and SoC portfolio with a <a href="https://www.altera.com/newsroom/agilex-2x">hardware root of trust, secure boot and attestation</a>, PQC-ready crypto, and line-rate encryption for Ethernet, PCIe and memory.</li>
<li><strong>Google</strong> introduced <a href="https://blog.google/innovation-and-ai/models-and-research/gemini-models/introducing-gemini-4-carbon/">Gemini 4 Carbon</a>, its latest frontier model with cybersecurity capabilities.</li>
</ul>
<h3><strong>Security technical papers</strong></h3>
<ul>
<li><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.36612">Fault Vulnerability of HW</a> Malware Detectors (<strong>UC Davis</strong> et al)</li>
<li>Detection &amp; classification of <a href="https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s00521-026-12467-4">RISC-V HW exploits</a> using ML (<strong>UPC</strong>)</li>
<li>Bringing <a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.36219">Hardware Security Education</a> to All (<strong>U. of Kansas, UF</strong> et al.)</li>
<li>Provably Secure Data <a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.37454">Memory-Dependent Prefetching</a> (<strong>DistriNet, KU Leuven</strong>)</li>
<li><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.33748">Rowhammer attack</a> on Fil-C&#8217;s software capabilities (Independent researcher and <strong>U. Sussex</strong>)</li>
</ul>
<hr />
<h2 id="Trending Video"><strong>Trending Video</strong></h2>
<p><a href="https://semiengineering.com/why-proof-convergence-matters/">Why Proof Convergence Matters</a>: Leveraging patterns in formal verification to reach sign-off faster. Ashish Darbari, CEO of <strong>Axiomise</strong>, talks about the impact of functional interactions involving safety and security, where to look for common patterns to prevent bugs from creeping into these designs, and how this all can be used to reduce time to sign-off with higher coverage.</p>
<p><iframe src="https://www.youtube.com/embed/EEsCDMSRKoY" width="480" height="270" frameborder="0" allowfullscreen="allowfullscreen"></iframe></p>
<hr />
<h2 id="Quantum Hardware"><strong>Quantum Hardware</strong></h2>
<p><strong>IBM</strong> demonstrated <a href="https://www.ibm.com/quantum/blog/connecting-quantum-processors-with-l-couplers">four interconnects linking two Nighthawk quantum processors</a>, achieving 99% average quantum state-transfer fidelity and 94.75% two-qubit gate fidelity, advancing modular quantum computing.</p>
<p><strong>NVision Quantum</strong> demonstrated a <a href="https://www.nvision-quantum.com/blog/nvision-unveils-first-manufacturable-molecular-qubit">single-molecule spin-photon interface</a> and partnered with HyperLight to integrate molecular qubits onto photonic chips using established semiconductor fabrication processes.</p>
<p><strong>FZ Jülich</strong>-led researchers demonstrated <a href="https://www.fz-juelich.de/en/news/archive/highlights/2026/speed-record-for-quantum-gates">6.8-nanosecond single-qubit gates</a> on a superconducting transmon, achieving 99.981% and 99.989% fidelity. Precision pulse shaping suppressed unwanted transitions, keeping leakage below 0.002% per operation.</p>
<p>A <strong>Technical University of Denmark</strong> team demonstrated integrated <a href="https://www.nature.com/articles/s41467-026-78806-0">quantum light sources using 2D materials</a>, coupling WSe₂ single-photon emitters to WS₂ waveguides on a chip, with an estimated minimum waveguide-coupled rate of 1.68 million photons per second.</p>
<hr />
<h2 id="Workforce, Education"><strong>Workforce, Education</strong></h2>
<p data-pm-slice="1 1 []">In a new special report, Semiconductor Engineering examines how <a href="https://semiengineering.com/ai-raises-the-bar-for-tomorrows-engineers/">AI is changing the skills tomorrow’s engineers</a> will need, with universities placing greater emphasis on strong math and physics fundamentals, critical thinking, and the ability to validate AI-generated results. Semiconductor employers say graduates will need both AI fluency and enough technical depth to recognize when AI gets things wrong.</p>
<p data-pm-slice="1 1 []"><strong>Northeastern University</strong> developed a <a href="https://ece.northeastern.edu/news/northeastern-team-earns-second-place-in-ieee-metaverse-grand-challenge-for-vr-and-hologram-based-semiconductor-training/">VR and holographic training platform for semiconductor manufacturing</a> that lets one student operate a simulated nanofabrication furnace used to grow nanowires while other students follow the process on a 3D display.</p>
<p><strong>UCSD</strong> and <strong>TSMC</strong> launched a new <a href="https://today.ucsd.edu/story/uc-san-diego-partners-with-tsmc-to-create-pipeline-from-the-classroom-to-semiconductor-cleanroom">hands-on semiconductor training program</a> for undergraduates.</p>

		<title>Auto-Generation Of Properties: Methodology Insertion</title>
		<link>https://semiengineering.com/auto-generation-of-properties-methodology-insertion/</link>
					<comments>https://semiengineering.com/auto-generation-of-properties-methodology-insertion/#respond</comments>
		
		<dc:creator><![CDATA[Brian Bailey]]></dc:creator>
		<pubDate>Thu, 29 Oct 2026 07:13:12 +0000</pubDate>
				<category><![CDATA[System-Level Design]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Top Stories]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[AI]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Arteris]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[assertions]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Axiomise]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Cadence]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[EDA]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[formal verification]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Normal Computing]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[RAG]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Synopsys]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[University of Southampton]]></category>
		<guid isPermaLink="false">https://semiengineering.com/?p=24283346</guid>

					<description><![CDATA[<p>Technology is only useful if it improves an existing methodology. Property generation is still refining that goal, but in the long term it could be very useful.</p>
<p>The post <a href="https://semiengineering.com/auto-generation-of-properties-methodology-insertion/">Auto-Generation Of Properties: Methodology Insertion</a> appeared first on <a href="https://semiengineering.com">Semiconductor Engineering</a>.</p>
]]></description>
										<content:encoded><![CDATA[<p><strong>Key Takeaways:</strong></p>
<ul>
<li>While AI can write properties from a specification, the specifications that exist today are rarely good enough to be able to generate anything useful from them.</li>
<li>Automatic generation of properties does not reduce the need for people who are fluent in the language, or the application of formal tools.</li>
<li>Introduction into a methodology will probably be different for each company today, with no well-proven methodologies to learn from.</li>
</ul>
<hr />
<p>A top-down semiconductor development flow has long been a goal for chip design, and shift left is certainly making that increasingly possible. The problem is that the starting point — the specification — is often incomplete or nonexistent, because the chip industry has been notoriously bad at writing specifications.</p>
<p>There are several ways to insert AI into existing flows. But just because something can be done doesn&#8217;t mean it should be. This is one of the big challenges facing AI adoption. Many existing tasks could be done more efficiently using an agentic flow, but it&#8217;s not clear whether that is the right thing to do.</p>
<p>This limitation becomes especially apparent when AI starts to help with something that has been woefully inadequate in the past — the creation of good, complete specifications. The big question is whether AI can enable designs, or verification environments, to be automatically derived from a specification. Perhaps a more correct goal is whether AI can help create better specifications. With better specifications, downstream design becomes a lot easier.</p>
<p>&#8220;AI can generate a SystemVerilog assertion in seconds,&#8221; says Ben Cohen, verification technologist and book author. &#8220;Whether that assertion means anything depends entirely on what it was generated from, and in practice that source is rarely a single, clean, natural-language specification. It is just as often an ambiguous paragraph that needs clarifying before it can be trusted.&#8221;</p>
<p>Solving this problem should be considered a high priority. &#8220;The increasing complexity of modern SoC designs is making AI-assisted artefact generation and verification closure a practical requirement rather than a research option,&#8221; says Simon Davidmann, AI and EDA researcher at the University of Southampton. &#8220;The verification problem is, in practice, already at the scale where it cannot be addressed without AI assistance. The 5-to-1 ratio of verification engineers to designers in processor development is not a workforce planning anomaly, but a signal that the complexity has outgrown the methods.&#8221;</p>
<p>Going from specification to anything is not a reality today. &#8220;If we are actually going to make this reality, we need more discipline in terms of writing the spec,&#8221; says Ravindra Aneja, director of applications engineering at <a href="https://semiengineering.com/entities/synopsys-inc/">Synopsys</a>. &#8220;If we really want to make it a reality, we have to work on it. As an industry, are we there? No, we are not there. I don&#8217;t think we are at a place where, either starting a new design or doing a derivative design, you can say the spec is good enough.&#8221;</p>
<p>Are we even thinking about it in the right way? &#8220;A prose specification document is not something a formal tool can reason over directly, because formal engines operate on precise, unambiguous, machine-checkable statements, not natural language,&#8221; says Ashish Darbari, CEO of <a href="https://semiengineering.com/entities/axiomise/">Axiomise</a>. &#8220;What AI can help with is narrowing that gap, translating prose into candidate formal statements faster, which is really what deriving properties from specification means in practice. The properties are the formalization of the spec, not a separate step after it. So rather than thinking of it as spec first, then properties, then formal proof, it is more accurate to say the property set is the machine-readable spec, and formal is working on the spec once that translation has happened.&#8221;</p>
<p>Sometimes, specifications are purposely ambiguous. &#8220;Human designers interpret them with domain knowledge, prior experience, and the ability to ask clarifying questions,&#8221; says Southampton&#8217;s Davidmann. &#8220;AI models receive the specification text and generate output without the ability to flag ambiguity or identify inconsistency. Specification ambiguity is likely a significant contributor to LLM-generated code failures, but the current research literature does not yet systematically characterize how often this is the root cause, as distinct from model capability limits. The next step is developing methods for automated specification analysis that can identify ambiguity before it enters the generation pipeline.&#8221;</p>
<p>Should the problem be looked at the other way around? &#8220;Rather than having formal work on specs directly, there is a strong argument that modern spec authorship should be formal and machine-readable by construction,&#8221; says Arvind Srinivasan, design verification solutions engineer at Normal Computing. &#8220;This is the only way to solve the inherent problems of trust, coverage, and drift. This is structurally impossible using only existing formal methodologies, but is achievable if you can use novel solvers and languages like Lean.&#8221;</p>
<p>That can feed the long-term goal. &#8220;I want to give a spec to my large language model, and I want it to generate code,&#8221; says Paul Graykowski, director of product marketing at <a href="https://semiengineering.com/entities/cadence-design-systems/">Cadence</a>. &#8220;But how do you capture the true intent of what a spec is, and make sure that is what you&#8217;re using as your single source of truth? There are a lot of things that must be explored, things that existing specifications just take for granted.&#8221;</p>
<p>There is probably room for an iterative approach. &#8220;When you read RTL, it can give you some abstract information about what your RTL is trying to do,&#8221; says Synopsys&#8217; Aneja. &#8220;It may not be perfect, but it can give you a good starting point, and then you can modify that spec in an incremental manner. You can go from there. It&#8217;s not perfect, but at least it gives you a reasonable starting point.&#8221;</p>
<p><strong>Skills migration</strong><br />
One of the supposed advantages of auto-generated properties is that it would reduce the need for specialized verification people. This may not be the case.</p>
<p>&#8220;For teams without internal formal expertise, complex AI-generated formal properties can be a boondoggle to review and maintain, and there are well-understood boundaries to the utility of SystemVerilog assertion-based methodologies,&#8221; says Normal&#8217;s Srinivasan. &#8220;The key principle is internal consistency. How can heavily AI-driven design processes leverage formal as both a check against other AI-driven design and verification collateral, but also utilize other collateral as a check against spurious or incorrect formal properties? Users that place stable systems of record and internal consistency at the heart of their processes will be better placed to utilize these systems in a zero-trust fashion.&#8221;</p>
<p>Someone still must read each generated SVA property. &#8220;They need to judge whether the antecedent is too weak or too tight, whether the consequent captures the actual requirement, whether clocking and reset conditions are handled correctly, and whether the property could pass vacuously,&#8221; says Axiomise&#8217;s Darbari. &#8220;That requires fluency in temporal logic and assertion semantics, as well as deep understanding of micro-architecture. Without that skill, a team has no way to tell a strong property from a weak one that happens to look similar, and they will not know how to interpret a formal tool&#8217;s counterexample or a &#8216;proven&#8217; result correctly either. So the practical requirement is that AI lowers the writing burden but raises the importance of having formal-literate engineers in the loop to interpret and validate the output. Organizations adopting this should budget for that expertise rather than assuming the tooling removes the need for it.&#8221;</p>
<p>There are some roles where formal properties may be more beneficial than others. &#8220;Formal verification is becoming a more important part of the solution,&#8221; says Ashley Stevens, director of product management and marketing at <a href="https://semiengineering.com/entities/arterisip/">Arteris</a>. &#8220;It is particularly strong for architectural compliance — ensuring ISA properties hold for all legal instruction sequences — and for enforcing correctness of hardware protocols in implementation verification. But formal is not a replacement for dynamic verification or system-level validation, especially when full SoC behavior and real workloads are involved. In practice, formal methods play a complementary role, proving deep corner-case correctness, while simulation and emulation establish end-to-end completeness.&#8221;</p>
<p><strong>Problem areas</strong><br />
Vacuous assertions are a specific accuracy risk that deserves direct attention. &#8220;A vacuous assertion is an SVA that compiles correctly, passes the formal tool, and covers exactly nothing, because it is always true regardless of what the design does,&#8221; says Davidmann. &#8220;Vacuous assertions are not the same as correct assertions. A model that produces plausible-looking but vacuous SVAs provides false confidence while consuming formal tool capacity. A version of this technology that looks accurate on paper and is genuinely dangerous in practice is the one where the model is confident and wrong, the testbench was also generated by the model, and no one in the loop had the domain knowledge to notice. This is not a hypothetical failure mode. It is a workflow that is easy to construct inadvertently.&#8221;</p>
<p>There are significant costs associated with them. &#8220;In a formal verification flow, these errors are expensive,&#8221; says Cohen. &#8220;They consume tool runtime, engineer debug time, and — most critically — erode confidence in the verification results themselves.&#8221;</p>
<p>Formal verification involves multiple types of solvers, one of which is satisfiability modulo theories (SMTs). These look at determining whether a mathematical formula is satisfiable. &#8220;For agentic systems that put formal tools in the loop, there is a non-trivial cost from SMT solver runs,&#8221; says Srinivasan. &#8220;In addition, these scale with the complexity of the design and number of properties.&#8221;</p>
<p><strong>Getting started</strong><br />
Deciding how to start using properties as part of a methodology is not obvious, and there are several issues that must be considered. There is also no single methodology that has been well tested, and it is likely that every company may need to work out what gives them the best balance of skills, time, and compute commitment, and the highest degree of verification closure.</p>
<p>AI needs to learn how generated properties are going to be used. &#8220;There are assertions for simulation and assertion for formal,&#8221; says Aneja. &#8220;There are techniques where you can write formal friendly properties, and that helps you with scaling in terms of, &#8216;Can you get a definite answer depending on how you&#8217;re writing the property?&#8217; When you&#8217;re trying to write these properties, rather than writing a very long property, you want to divide that function into multiple smaller parts. That gives you scalability. Then you&#8217;re not getting stuck on a behavior that takes 200 cycles to do anything meaningful because it won&#8217;t work in formal.&#8221;</p>
<p>&#8220;Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) offers a principled solution,&#8221; says Cohen. &#8220;Rather than relying solely on what an LLM learned during training, a RAG-enhanced system queries a curated knowledge base at generation time — retrieving the specific rules, patterns, and lessons that apply to the task at hand. RAG rules represent years of accumulated application knowledge — rules derived from real projects, real tool behaviors, and real failures. They encode not just what is syntactically correct, but what is verifiably sound; the difference between an assumption that constrains the environment appropriately and one that makes the formal proof space unreachable; between a property that checks the right behavior and one that passes vacuously; between timing diagrams that match the generated code and ones that silently diverge.&#8221;</p>
<p>There are stepped approaches that could be tried. &#8220;There is an intermediate step called formal test plan, where if you have a perfect spec, it talks about what your design does, not what you want to verify,&#8221; says Aneja. &#8220;The idea is that with all these LLMs and agents, you have the ability to read text, look at timing diagrams, look at FSM diagrams, other pictures you may have, and then you can extract certain features based on the designs you are doing. And you can train your system to say, is it a CPU design? Is it a GPU design? Is it an AI accelerator? Go one level down, is it a memory controller? Is it an interrupt controller? Is it a bus bridge? Or is it some interconnect across different managers and subordinates? Depending on what that is, it can extract certain features that you may want to test.&#8221;</p>
<p>The same considerations go for choosing an LLM. &#8220;Domain boundary awareness needs to become a first-class engineering discipline,&#8221; says Davidmann. &#8220;Before adopting any AI model into a production flow, the right question is not only how accurate the model is, but what it was trained on, what that distribution does not cover, and how close the actual design is to that boundary. Every team deploying these tools should run a deliberate boundary-probing exercise on a representative sample of their most complex and novel design patterns, verifying the outputs independently against what a domain expert would produce. That exercise identifies where the distribution ends and the model uncertainty begins. Without it, deployment is proceeding without the information needed to manage the risk.&#8221;</p>
<p>Start small and build confidence. &#8220;Pilot on a bounded, well-understood block first, compare the AI output against an existing hand-written property set, and measure the gap before rolling this out broadly,&#8221; says Darbari. &#8220;Finally, this only works well alongside engineers who already understand formal methods. It is not a way to skip that expertise. It is a way to make expert engineers faster. This is one area where I&#8217;m seeing an interesting demand from bigger design houses. Management wants engineers trained in formal so they can drive the entire ecosystem of tools and apply methodology properly.&#8221;</p>
<p>It is also important that you consider the domain in which you are working. &#8220;Tooling is currently ahead of the governance,&#8221; says Davidmann. &#8220;Most teams do not yet have a standard for documenting which assertions are human-authored, and which are AI-generated, or what that distinction means for sign-off confidence. In addition, most vendor documentation does not specify the training scope, failure modes, or domain boundaries of the models being deployed. Both gaps need to close before AI-generated content scales into safety-critical designs.&#8221;</p>
<p>The post <a href="https://semiengineering.com/auto-generation-of-properties-methodology-insertion/">Auto-Generation Of Properties: Methodology Insertion</a> appeared first on <a href="https://semiengineering.com">Semiconductor Engineering</a>.</p>
]]></content:encoded>

		<title>Rethinking Shift Left In Chip Design</title>
		<link>https://semiengineering.com/rethinking-shift-left-in-chip-design/</link>
					<comments>https://semiengineering.com/rethinking-shift-left-in-chip-design/#respond</comments>
		
		<dc:creator><![CDATA[Ed Sperling]]></dc:creator>
		<pubDate>Thu, 29 Oct 2026 07:11:57 +0000</pubDate>
				<category><![CDATA[Round Tables]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[System-Level Design]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Top Stories]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[agentic AI]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[AI trust]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Axiomise]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Hiveware]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[shift left]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Silvaco]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Synopsys]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[validation]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[verification]]></category>
		<guid isPermaLink="false">https://semiengineering.com/?p=24283437</guid>

					<description><![CDATA[<p>Is it better to do everything earlier in the flow, or should design be more flexible and iterative? </p>
<p>The post <a href="https://semiengineering.com/rethinking-shift-left-in-chip-design/">Rethinking Shift Left In Chip Design</a> appeared first on <a href="https://semiengineering.com">Semiconductor Engineering</a>.</p>
]]></description>
										<content:encoded><![CDATA[<div>
<div>
<p><strong>Key Takeaways:</strong></p>
<ul>
<li>AI agents and digital twins are making it possible to explore more design options earlier, including choices involving materials, transistor structures, and chip architectures.</li>
<li>Moving work earlier doesn&#8217;t eliminate verification bottlenecks. Engineers still need to establish whether AI-generated designs and surrogate models accurately reflect the intended hardware.</li>
<li>Shift left is becoming less linear. Instead of locking down specifications early, design teams may be able to refine architectures and implementation choices as new information becomes available.</li>
</ul>
</div>
</div>
<hr />
<p><strong>Experts at the Table</strong>: Semiconductor Engineering sat down to discuss the good and bad of shifting left amid ongoing changes in how chips are designed, used, and updated throughout the flow, with Frank Schirrmeister, executive director of strategy programs and system solutions at <a href="https://semiengineering.com/entities/synopsys-inc/">Synopsys</a>; Ronen Laviv, vice president of business development and strategic partnerships at Hiveware; Jack Berg, vice president of business development at <a href="https://semiengineering.com/entities/silvaco-inc/">Silvaco</a>; Ashish Darbari, founder and CEO of <a href="https://semiengineering.com/entities/axiomise/">Axiomise</a>. What follows are excerpts of that conversation.</p>
<p><img data-recalc-dims="1" decoding="async" class="alignnone size-full wp-image-24283440" src="https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/10/Screenshot-2026-10-28-at-3.04.41-PM.png?resize=1716%2C538&#038;ssl=1" alt="" width="1716" height="538" srcset="https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/10/Screenshot-2026-10-28-at-3.04.41-PM.png?w=1716&amp;ssl=1 1716w, https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/10/Screenshot-2026-10-28-at-3.04.41-PM.png?resize=300%2C94&amp;ssl=1 300w, https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/10/Screenshot-2026-10-28-at-3.04.41-PM.png?resize=1024%2C321&amp;ssl=1 1024w, https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/10/Screenshot-2026-10-28-at-3.04.41-PM.png?resize=768%2C241&amp;ssl=1 768w, https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/10/Screenshot-2026-10-28-at-3.04.41-PM.png?resize=1536%2C482&amp;ssl=1 1536w, https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/10/Screenshot-2026-10-28-at-3.04.41-PM.png?resize=600%2C188&amp;ssl=1 600w" sizes="(max-width: 1000px) 100vw, 1000px" /><br />
<strong>L-R: Silvaco&#8217;s Berg; Synopsys&#8217; Schirrmeister; Axiomise&#8217;s Darbari; Hiveware&#8217;s Laviv.</strong></p>
<p><strong>SE: We&#8217;ve been talking about shifting left in the design-through-manufacturing flow for more than a decade, but more and more capabilities are being added to improve reliability and yield, and produce silicon faster. What changes are you seeing?</strong></p>
<p><strong>Schirrmeister</strong>: We&#8217;re now adding digital twins, which are shifting very left.</p>
<p><strong>Berg</strong>: That&#8217;s correct. And the issue now is working with multi-physics models. Using that data to assemble surrogate models is important. As we go further into different technologies with different materials, you have to go back to the materials science solvers and physics solvers to get models that designers can use. This is happening in areas that are not typically digital design, but as we get down to the CFET level, it&#8217;s starting to be used there, as well.</p>
<p><strong>Schirrmeister</strong>: There are three changes. Number one, now we have the technical ability with compute, with agents, to shift much further left than we ever hoped we would get to in our lifetimes. The problem there is whether shift left is trustworthy. Is the surrogate model a reliable representation of what&#8217;s happening? You can&#8217;t have the agent decide that. In the past, for virtual prototyping, you would get a human being who is responsible for ensuring that the model represents what&#8217;s happening, and that it&#8217;s accurate. That&#8217;s number one. So the trust is changing. Number two, you need to trust the shift-left model to be predictive of what you&#8217;re doing. Otherwise, you&#8217;ll regret you&#8217;re shifting left because you&#8217;ll need another spin. In a Qualcomm keynote, the speaker talked about the new reasoning gap. He said we used to have a productivity gap, but now we have a reasoning gap. They want engineers to reason about the product and make that product better. But the tools can&#8217;t deal with reasoning yet. The third one is the rapid pace of shift-left options. If I get to the point where I can trust the models, then I have new things I can do. At the architectural level, I can decide something and kick off &#8216;n&#8217; options to try it out if the models are predictive enough.</p>
<p><strong>Darbari</strong>: The shift-left discourse started at least 15 years ago. What has changed is AI and the agentic use model. What has not happened yet is the trust element in today&#8217;s AI agents. There are all these excellent use models for the agents, which we use and our customers use, to go through the specifications. A lot of discussion has now moved into spec-to-silicon, because people believe you can extract requirements from specification documents — if you have them. But most of the time you don&#8217;t have them, and that is one of the problems. Another big change I&#8217;m seeing in the work we do with formal verification is that functional verification is now seen as a problem, at the same level as security, functional safety, and power, with all of the hardware feeding into the AI ecosystem, which is the largest investment pool. A lot of companies are investing in PPA analysis on the first day. For example, I know of customers that are taking their designs, even the older generation, and saying, &#8216;Let an AI agent rewrite it for optimized PPA.&#8217; But how do I know it is correct? Can I prove it to be equivalent to something else? So the big 10 companies in the semiconductor space, which have big budgets and big teams of people, are adopting agentic AI flows, and they&#8217;re heavily leaning on whether they can trust it or not trust it. That trust gap is often being solved through early adoption of formal through proofs. The know-how is not necessarily the biggest blocker. It&#8217;s mostly whether tools can integrate more seamlessly into the system.</p>
<p>But if you look at what is enabling more shift left, it&#8217;s the time spent on debug. When you&#8217;re bringing up an architectural analysis and you have a failure, you can feed it to an agent. There&#8217;s a lot of data coming out of the agents, and we need to do more work on insights. Are you spending your time wisely? Even if it is correct or incorrect, if a manual review has to happen, that could be a massive blocker in the early phases of adoption of shift left. With agents being adopted earlier, will this lead to better designs with fewer bug escapes? That remains to be seen, and I don&#8217;t believe that shift left alone will be able to fix it.</p>
<p><strong>Laviv</strong>: Shift left is identifying the bottlenecks in our industry and looking at what you can do in order to pull in work and schedule as much as you can earlier in the design and verification cycle. Nowadays, the agents are leaping forward to help, but everything that happens gets us into bottlenecks — new bottlenecks or old bottlenecks — that we have to solve.</p>
<p><strong>SE: We have so much that has to be looked at and verified concurrently that it becomes a problem to shift that much stuff left, and it gets more complicated with multi-die assemblies and lifecycle management. How do all the pieces go together and stay together?</strong></p>
<p><strong>Schirrmeister</strong>: Is shift left even the right thing? Shift left assumes that I have a solid knowledge of what I&#8217;m actually going to do, and it assumes I can specify what I want six or nine months or a year from now. Maybe we&#8217;re thinking about this wrong. Perhaps what we need is a general direction, and then the ability to make adjustments based on the information we gain as we go toward implementation choices. Shift left implies that you can get to zero in some magic future world by having something spec&#8217;d, and you get all the way there. What I hear people doing is different. They say, &#8216;Okay, I&#8217;m building something, and with agentic AI I can now build this model that potentially represents most of it. So now I make the decision, &#8216;This one is fairly straightforward and it doesn&#8217;t need that much effort on an Arm processor, or I can make a RISC-V processor for it.&#8217; Now I reset my spec, which is kind of messed up because I did not have the correct hardware spec, so now I&#8217;m making adjustments. The cool part is we are approaching a time when we can shift left to this new refinement. It&#8217;s almost like an agile shift left. In an agile way, you make adjustments to your implementation for micro choices that happen later based on what you&#8217;re doing now.</p>
<p><strong>SE: That addresses a big problem with these designs, because you don&#8217;t know what the workload will be by the time this chip is deployed in the market. This is a more generic approach that focuses on adaptability, right?</strong></p>
<p><strong>Schirrmeister</strong>: Exactly, and we didn&#8217;t dare do this in the past because this was all sequential. Now it becomes more circular. So there was a product manager who knew the market and said, &#8216;I&#8217;m building this, and then 18 months later you found out it didn&#8217;t work.&#8217; Right now you have new data all the time, so there can be this agility in refining what you&#8217;re doing.</p>
<p><strong>Darbari</strong>: I agree that there are lots of choices in agility and lots of changes, and not everything is known up front, which is the reality in building anything. However, I do not see that things are so fuzzy. If you&#8217;re building a new machine learning chip, or a GPU or a CPU, you have some idea of what the derivative design was before. What is the general architecture you&#8217;re going to play with? What you don&#8217;t know is what the implementation choices are going to be for power, for functionality, and with AI bringing lots of challenges on training or inference. Those are quite novel. So you need to make multiple choices at every step along the way, with or without agents. But agents can help you do that more efficiently. What I don&#8217;t know is whether the outcomes of these agile agents can be reconciled very quickly and trusted.</p>
<p><strong>Schirrmeister</strong>: You don&#8217;t let AI actually make the decision for you, and you don&#8217;t trust the AI. You then need to go down into a verified model.</p>
<p><strong>Berg</strong>: That&#8217;s a really important point. The AI agents don&#8217;t replace humans. They are effectively junior engineers, and they need to have their work reviewed by somebody who knows it. The notion that it&#8217;s agile, and is growing agile, is very real. There&#8217;s this notion that a flow does an inverse design. But what happens if that inverse design could be based on multi-physics solvers. You put together a surrogate model, digital twins, and then you say, &#8216;Okay, give me a transistor or give me a chip — or even so far as a chip through RTL at this point — that works with these constraints and these things that are going on. And all of a sudden you have an empty set. It&#8217;s over-constrained. Now what do you do? This is why the notion that you have to be agile is very valid.</p>
<p><strong>Laviv</strong>: If we take the example of the digital flow, you have virtual platforms today, formal, software simulation, emulation, FPGA, and you have engineers and AI engines. You will need to make adjustments. And if you&#8217;re designing a CPU, or something that&#8217;s tangible, and you can do it better with formal and bring it to the table sooner with a more stable design, then emulation and FPGA can run sooner in the pipeline of doing the verification stuff, and the same thing with simulation. If you have done something to solve the bottlenecks in formal, in simulation, virtual prototyping, or even the flow itself, this would help shift everything left and allow you to make better decisions earlier in the game. That way if you need to change stuff, you can change it.</p>
<p><strong>Darbari</strong>: The validation problem is significant. I&#8217;m seeing real evidence with some customers, who literally are going back to the drawing board to build a ground-up architecture that can address different phases of their chips being used. So they could build a CPU, a GPU, a machine learning chip, and they really need to scope out whether what they intend to build would cover all kinds of use models. That&#8217;s not easy, especially when you bring software into the loop, and the software isn&#8217;t what used to run 20 years ago. There is training, inference, power coming into the chain all the way back to the architecture level, and then you&#8217;ve got to make these choices.</p>
<p>The post <a href="https://semiengineering.com/rethinking-shift-left-in-chip-design/">Rethinking Shift Left In Chip Design</a> appeared first on <a href="https://semiengineering.com">Semiconductor Engineering</a>.</p>
]]></content:encoded>
