					<description><![CDATA[<p>Universities are redesigning curricula and assessments around judgment, validation, and technical depth. </p>
<p>The post <a href="https://semiengineering.com/ai-raises-the-bar-for-tomorrows-engineers/">AI Raises The Bar For Tomorrow’s Engineers</a> appeared first on <a href="https://semiengineering.com">Semiconductor Engineering</a>.</p>
]]></description>
										<content:encoded><![CDATA[<p><strong>Key Takeaways:</strong></p>
<ul>
<li>Universities are integrating AI into engineering education while reinforcing physics, math, and other fundamentals that students need to evaluate AI-generated work.</li>
<li>Critical thinking and engineering judgment are more important than ever because AI can produce plausible but incorrect answers, leaving students responsible for verifying results and assumptions.</li>
<li>Schools are redesigning assessments around oral exams, in-class quizzes, labs, projects, and other tasks that test genuine understanding while allowing responsible AI use.</li>
</ul>
<hr />
<p>Today’s science, technology, engineering, and math (STEM) students will be entering the workforce at a pivotal moment. They grew up on iPads, attended high school classes on Zoom during the COVID pandemic, and are navigating college amid an AI revolution. Now, with generative and agentic tools able to accelerate many tasks, a burning question remains: How can universities adapt to this new era and help students harness AI’s advantages without weakening the foundational knowledge and judgment they will need as engineers?</p>
<p>Academic institutions are working to help students get the most out of AI tools while ensuring the next generation of engineers doesn&#8217;t cut corners on foundational knowledge. One trend is universities placing greater emphasis on critical-thinking tasks so students don&#8217;t swallow GenAI slop in totality. Just as large language models need guardrails, universities are developing tests and quizzes to ensure students learn properly and to minimize cheating. [See <a href="#Table of University Approaches">Table of University Approaches</a> below.]</p>
<p>“We are seeing people who skate through or miss something fundamental that was important for a particular job, and they have to go back and relearn it,&#8221; said Alexandra Werth, assistant professor in biomedical engineering at Cornell University. &#8220;Higher ed is in trouble if we don&#8217;t work hard to make sure that doesn&#8217;t happen. I just went to an American Society of Engineering Education conference, where there were thousands of engineering educators, and this was what everyone wanted to talk about and figure out because this is what makes education relevant. People are trying a lot of different things, and we&#8217;re still in a weird place where AI technology is constantly changing. Saying, &#8216;This is the right approach,&#8217; or &#8216;This is the wrong approach,&#8217; is still in flux.&#8221;</p>
<p>Students are adopting AI tools faster than industry and academia can determine the right balance. “Everybody&#8217;s kind of struggling with it, to be honest,” said Matthew Ozalas, master application development engineer, scientist at <a href="https://semiengineering.com/entities/keysight-technologies/">Keysight</a>. “I&#8217;ve talked to professors and heard a lot of different perspectives across the gamut. Some professors are saying, ‘No technology in my class — just blue books back to the old days.&#8217; Others say, ‘Use whatever you want, but you have a time limit.’ I&#8217;ve been really impressed with how AI-savvy young people are. At the same time, there’s this idea about how much they need to know in the engineering field. People have asked, &#8216;Do they even need to know everything anymore? Can&#8217;t they just ask their large language model?'&#8221;</p>
<p>That means validation of AI responses is key. &#8220;The biggest shift we&#8217;re seeing is that next-generation engineers need to be good at defining problems, providing the right context to AI, validating its output, and understanding tradeoffs,&#8221; said William Wang, founder and CEO of <a href="https://semiengineering.com/entities/alpha-design-ai-chipagents/">ChipAgents</a>. &#8220;Engineering judgment has become even more important, not less, because engineers are now responsible for validating AI-generated work, recognizing incorrect assumptions, and knowing when deeper investigation is required.”</p>
<p>That uncertainty only raises the stakes for mastering the fundamentals, especially when AI can produce answers that sound convincing but are wrong. &#8220;From an industry perspective, it is strongly recommended that universities should teach students how to combine fundamentals with AI-assisted workflows,&#8221; said Andy Nightingale, vice president of product management and marketing at <a href="https://semiengineering.com/entities/arterisip/">Arteris</a>. &#8220;Specify the problem clearly, generate alternatives, validate assumptions, test results, and document decisions. The graduates who will stand out are not those who use AI, but those who can use it safely, critically, and productively.&#8221;</p>
<p>Students also must be able to defend their findings orally, without AI help. &#8220;Graduates need soft skills tailored for a generation who have grown up on screens,&#8221; said Ashish Darbari, CEO of <a href="https://semiengineering.com/entities/axiomise/">Axiomise</a>. &#8220;In industry, engineers have to explain their reasoning on the spot — in design reviews, with customers, and when something goes wrong. Clear, confident verbal communication matters more in the AI era because it shows thinking that AI can&#8217;t produce for you. Universities should make it a deliberate part of the curriculum.&#8221;</p>
<p>In short, students still need the knowledge and judgment to evaluate what an LLM tells them.</p>
<p>Experience makes the risks clear. “If you work with AI and you&#8217;re experienced, you can see how confidently it gives you completely wrong answers or will hallucinate its way through,” said Ozalas. “It’s the balancing act that we have to strike. I can imagine it&#8217;s really hard right now to be a student or a young person trying to get into this.”</p>
<p>One professor, who spoke on condition he not be named, pointed to a potentially more disturbing issue. &#8220;AI is a resource that has been helping facilitate student research and learning over the past several years,&#8221; he said. &#8220;However, over this period, I have observed an overall decline in student retention and performance in the same type of curriculum, suggesting that the benefits of AI may be outweighed by increased reliance on this tool. The overall trend has appeared to be that students are having decreasing attention spans as AI becomes more integrated into education and knowledge transfer.”</p>
<p>Another professor, speaking on condition of anonymity, declined to discuss students’ use of AI. Her reason: “Because then I’ll be bitter.”</p>
<p>Universities must address such concerns. &#8220;It is essential for university curricula to target GenAI weaknesses,&#8221; said Tamara Popovic, educational developer for IT and electrical engineering at ETH Zurich. “One of the biggest challenges we face is what’s called the illusion of competence. When a student prompts an LLM to explain a concept like heterojunction formation, the tool generates a beautifully structured, highly confident response. The student reads it, feels an immediate sense of clarity, and mistakes that passive clarity for actual mastery. They skip the difficult mental strain required to truly internalize the physics behind the phenomena. To break that illusion of competence, we started to design learning activities that target the specific cognitive blind spots of GenAI, namely, complex spatial relationships and non-standard visual abstractions.&#8221;</p>
<p>For example, the university introduced exercises based on multi-variable graphic shifting. &#8220;We present a standard plot, like a MOSFET C-V curve, but introduce a unique physical anomaly,&#8221; said Popovic. &#8220;Text-based LLMs, for now, cannot accurately map these multi-layered physical transformations onto new visual representations without making fatal logical leaps. Forcing students to navigate these visual-spatial shifts ensures they are relying on their own internal mental models rather than an external algorithm.”</p>
<p>If AI can mimic understanding, universities must decide how to teach the real thing.</p>
<p><strong>Developing curricula in the AI era</strong><br />
Universities are figuring out how to allow AI tools while preserving academic rigor and integrity.</p>
<p>&#8220;Faculty is uncomfortable,&#8221; said Stanley Chan, professor of electrical and computer engineering and director of engineering AI education at Purdue University. “It&#8217;s the first time that we are navigating this path, and I tell administration and faculty members that we don&#8217;t have a crystal ball.&#8221;</p>
<p>The Purdue board recently approved a plan to integrate more AI into each successive year of a four-year degree. &#8220;We have to make the transformation because otherwise we will be outpaced by AI,&#8221; said Chan. &#8220;It&#8217;s a matter of time that higher education will be fundamentally reshaped because a new technology is here.&#8221;</p>
<p>To avoid loss of learning, professors have to ask students tough questions, and students need to learn how to ask AI tough questions, noted Chan. &#8220;Why are you doing this? Can you give me the evidence? Can you show me that this is working? Have you considered other design options? Why is this design done in this way, and have you thought about approaching the problem in another way? Maybe you should think about the other direction.&#8221;</p>
<p>Syllabi also need to strike the right balance. “There is that question of how to even write a good syllabus nowadays,” said David Janes, professor of electrical and computer engineering and the director of the semiconductor degrees program at Purdue. “In your syllabus you need to define what is and is not appropriate use of AI, and the answer is usually that you can&#8217;t use AI. If you use AI, you are still responsible for verifying and checking it. You are responsible for independently learning and understanding these concepts.”</p>
<p><img data-recalc-dims="1" loading="lazy" decoding="async" class="alignnone size-full wp-image-24283378" src="https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/10/Purdue-pic-2-scaled.jpeg?resize=2560%2C1707&#038;ssl=1" alt="" width="2560" height="1707" srcset="https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/10/Purdue-pic-2-scaled.jpeg?w=2560&amp;ssl=1 2560w, https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/10/Purdue-pic-2-scaled.jpeg?resize=300%2C200&amp;ssl=1 300w, https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/10/Purdue-pic-2-scaled.jpeg?resize=1024%2C683&amp;ssl=1 1024w, https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/10/Purdue-pic-2-scaled.jpeg?resize=768%2C512&amp;ssl=1 768w, https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/10/Purdue-pic-2-scaled.jpeg?resize=1536%2C1024&amp;ssl=1 1536w, https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/10/Purdue-pic-2-scaled.jpeg?resize=2048%2C1365&amp;ssl=1 2048w, https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/10/Purdue-pic-2-scaled.jpeg?resize=600%2C400&amp;ssl=1 600w" sizes="auto, (max-width: 1000px) 100vw, 1000px" /><br />
<strong>Fig. 1: A student and professor working in Purdue University’s Birck Nanotechnology Center. Source: Purdue</strong></p>
<p>Given this complex semiconductor work, AI fluency must be grounded in engineering fundamentals, whether students are tackling network-on-chip congestion or semiconductor test.</p>
<p>&#8220;AI can propose a fabric configuration in seconds, but knowing whether it will hold up under real traffic patterns, achieve physical implementation, and survive routing and other tradeoffs requires that underlying domain depth,&#8221; noted Nandan Nayampally, chief commercial officer at <a href="https://semiengineering.com/entities/baya-systems/">Baya Systems</a>. &#8220;That combination — AI plus real expertise — is where you get the actual productivity boost, not from AI alone. Without the fundamentals to validate it, AI-driven design accelerates disasters as fast as it accelerates progress.&#8221;</p>
<p>For semiconductor testing, students need deep, domain-specific knowledge that takes time to develop, observed Ryan Moran, head of talent acquisition at the Center of Excellence at <a href="https://semiengineering.com/entities/teradyne-corporation/">Teradyne</a>. &#8220;Universities that treat AI literacy as complementary to rigorous foundational training are producing the graduates we most want to hire. The more forward-thinking programs are redesigning assignments so that AI tool use is assumed. New graduates who arrive having used tools like GitHub Copilot, Claude Code, or similar assistants can produce working code drafts faster than earlier cohorts. The challenge is to maintain technical knowledge to both understand and verify what the code actually does.”</p>
<p>Foundational skills and deep disciplines such as physics and mathematics bring many job opportunities. &#8220;You can go very deep in linear algebra or calculus,&#8221; said Pradheepa Raman, chief people officer at GlobalFoundries. &#8220;If you go into the linear algebra side, you become a data scientist or AI engineer. If you go into the calculus side, you can become a quant in financial institutions. These old types of deep skills are in many curricula to help students become deep experts. I would prioritize that first because it&#8217;s hard for companies to teach students unless you have the basics.&#8221;</p>
<p>Ideally, graduates will know both science and AI. &#8220;The strongest candidates are those who know how to use AI as a productivity tool while also demonstrating solid engineering judgment, a problem-solving mindset, and a strong grasp of first principles,&#8221; said Gabriela Cruz Thompson, director of university research collaboration at <a href="https://semiengineering.com/entities/intel-foundry/">Intel</a>. &#8220;In other words, we are not looking for graduates who simply know how to prompt an AI model. We&#8217;re looking for graduates who understand the underlying science and engineering well enough to know when an AI-generated answer is correct, incomplete, or wrong.&#8221;</p>
<p>Producing those graduates requires assessments that reveal genuine understanding, not merely polished AI output.</p>
<p><strong>AI-proof assessments</strong><br />
While students can enter prompts for just about anything, from emails and job applications all the way to semiconductor designs, the most valuable new skill is not prompt writing by itself. &#8220;It is the ability to use AI as an engineering assistant while maintaining technical ownership of the result,” Arteris&#8217; Nightingale said. “The challenge for universities is to move beyond policing AI use and toward teaching responsible, verifiable engineering with AI in the loop.”</p>
<p>However, no universal silver bullet can prevent students from using AI to cheat. Assessments must be AI-proof, and professors need teaching assistants to help them adapt the curriculum course by course, Purdue&#8217;s Chan noted. &#8220;We need to ask the question, &#8216;Can AI solve all the homework problems by itself?&#8217; If the answer is yes, then the class itself has to be rethought. Otherwise, no matter how many countermeasures you put in, it&#8217;s going to be ineffective.&#8221;</p>
<p>That need for course-by-course redesign is widely shared. &#8220;AI can now answer most of the questions we used to set in exams and coursework, and universities are under pressure to make assessments AI-proof,&#8221; said Axiomise&#8217;s Darbari. &#8220;That isn&#8217;t only an assessment problem. It changes how students learn and what they are equipped with when they graduate. The risk is that we produce graduates who can get answers but can&#8217;t stand behind them.&#8221;</p>
<p>At the same time, students must still memorize some math and physics. &#8220;Some of the higher-level concepts they have to learn by heart,&#8221; said Chan. &#8220;We still want to keep some of the paper and pencil exams so that when they go to the design stage, they will know what questions to ask. That cannot come in a day.&#8221;</p>
<p>Creative assessments are also key, and some faculty hold in-class competitions. &#8220;There is an engineering design goal such as building a robot, and then there are targets, and you see who performs better,” he said. “You cannot cheat by just using AI. The robot has to physically run on the road.&#8221;</p>
<p>Students lose out if they take shortcuts. &#8220;At the Bachelor level, mastering a course such as semiconductor devices requires a massive amount of mental effort,” said ETH Zurich&#8217;s Popovic. “When students use generative AI as a cognitive shortcut, they bypass the very struggle that builds deep engineering intuition.”</p>
<p>Students using AI to cheat is not viewed differently from students copying others&#8217; code or written work. Purdue has used plagiarism checkers on graduate theses for quite some time to ensure students write their own work and cite appropriate sources, Purdue&#8217;s Janes says. &#8220;It&#8217;s a very parallel thing with AI, though we would go about it differently if it&#8217;s an engineering analysis than writing. We all struggle with the same problem.”</p>
<p>Physics cannot be traded for AI. “When engineers don&#8217;t know physics, they don&#8217;t know reality,&#8221; Keysight&#8217;s Ozalas said. &#8220;It’s the same with coding. If you don&#8217;t know coding and you just prompt everything, you lose the connection with the real world, and that&#8217;s a concern.”</p>
<p>Ultimately, traditional tests may still be best for fundamental knowledge. &#8220;Then provide more workshops, more projects, more labs, but definitely allow them to use the AI tools,&#8221; said GF&#8217;s Raman. &#8220;If you don&#8217;t, it&#8217;s like saying, &#8216;Hey, I have all these tools, but I want you to go the traditional way even though it will take enormous time.&#8217; Time is also of the essence. They can learn more if they are allowed to use the tools.&#8221;</p>
<p>All the company&#8217;s summer interns had access to AI tools, Raman noted. &#8220;They were amazing using these tokens. They were happy to get access and build things. It was awesome to see.&#8221;</p>
<p><strong>Conclusion</strong><br />
Like all new technology, AI in education brings pros and cons.</p>
<p>&#8220;AI has the potential to make both teaching and learning more effective, but only when it is designed and used thoughtfully,&#8221; said Ying Zhang, professor at Georgia Institute of Technology. &#8220;Like any educational technology, its impact depends on how educators and students use it. For instructors, AI can reduce time spent on repetitive tasks, such as generating practice problems or answering routine questions, allowing them to devote more time to mentoring students and designing engaging learning experiences. For students, AI can provide personalized explanations, timely feedback, and on-demand learning support, particularly outside of class and office hours.&#8221;</p>
<p>At the same time, there are legitimate challenges. &#8220;General-purpose AI systems can generate inaccurate or misleading information, often referred to as hallucinations,&#8221; said Zhang. &#8220;There is also a risk that students may become overly reliant on AI if it simply provides answers, reducing the cognitive effort that is essential for developing deep understanding and independent problem-solving abilities.&#8221;</p>
<p>Given the direction the industry is heading, denying students access to AI tools is pointless, even though the industry still hasn&#8217;t decided on the best approach to manage access. &#8220;From the AI perspective, you do need to build AI,&#8221; said Dana Neustadter, senior director of product management at <a href="https://semiengineering.com/entities/synopsys-inc/">Synopsys</a>. &#8220;Everything will have AI built in as a technology eventually, so you need AI security specialists to make sure that they build secure solutions for AI. AI tools might change the job landscape, but I don&#8217;t think positions will go away. It&#8217;s just that they&#8217;re going to change in their nature. To keep up with the technology, you&#8217;ll have to use AI tools and rely on AI assistance. I&#8217;ve heard from academia that there is a need for having specialized skill sets.&#8221; However, she added that the exact nature of these skills is still evolving.</p>
<p>This also means students need both hard knowledge and soft communication skills. &#8220;The graduates who do well in the AI era will be the ones who can use the tools, defend their reasoning, and communicate it clearly in the moment,&#8221; said Darbari. &#8220;Universities that build all of that into their curriculum will produce the engineers the semiconductor industry most needs.&#8221;</p>
<p>Darbari’s tips for universities:</p>
<ul>
<li>Guide students through the new way of learning.</li>
<li>Make students defend what they know.</li>
<li>Focus on applying knowledge, not recalling it.</li>
<li>Polish communication, especially spoken communication.</li>
<li>Build breadth and depth that don&#8217;t depend on AI.</li>
<li>Invest in expert academics.</li>
</ul>
<h3 id="Table of University Approaches"><strong>Table of University Approaches</strong></h3>
<p>The pace and depth of change in university curricula vary considerably across institutions. Both academia and industry benefit from open discussions about navigating the AI era.</p>

<td style="width: 22%; background: #F2F2F2; color: #000000; font-size: 13px; line-height: 1.3; text-align: left; vertical-align: top; padding: 4px 3px; border: 1px solid #17365D;">Critical evaluation; treat AI not as an authority, but as a flawed draft that requires expert auditing.</td>

<tr>

<td style="width: 19%; background: #FFFFFF; color: #000000; font-size: 13px; line-height: 1.3; text-align: left; vertical-align: top; padding: 4px 3px; border: 1px solid #17365D;">Per its <a style="color: #0563c1; text-decoration: underline;" href="https://oit.gatech.edu/ai/guidance" target="_blank" rel="noopener">AI policy and guidance</a>, encourages responsible, ethical use of AI to support teaching and learning; instructors encouraged to establish clear expectations for AI use and help students develop skills to use AI responsibly, ethically, and effectively.</td>
<td style="width: 20%; background: #FFFFFF; color: #000000; font-size: 13px; line-height: 1.3; text-align: left; vertical-align: top; padding: 4px 3px; border: 1px solid #17365D;">Strong engineering fundamentals and AI competency; focus on defining problems; evaluating and validating AI-generated results; integrating information from multiple sources; how to use AI tools effectively; when to trust AI vs. when to question outputs.</td>
<td style="width: 22%; background: #FFFFFF; color: #000000; font-size: 13px; line-height: 1.3; text-align: left; vertical-align: top; padding: 4px 3px; border: 1px solid #17365D;">Deeper learning, critical thinking, and responsible AI use; accountability for own understanding and work; exercise sound engineering judgment; communicate and collaborate effectively across multidisciplinary teams; critical thinking, creativity, ethical responsibility, and professional judgment central to engineering practice.</td>
<td style="width: 24%; background: #FFFFFF; color: #000000; font-size: 13px; line-height: 1.3; text-align: left; vertical-align: top; padding: 4px 3px; border: 1px solid #17365D;">In the school of ECE, developed <a style="color: #0563c1; text-decoration: underline;" href="https://ece.gatech.edu/news/2026/01/sleepless-tutor-turns-midnight-panic-progress" target="_blank" rel="noopener">SMART Tutor</a>, a course-specific AI tutor that combines RAG with principles of self-regulated learning; guides students through the problem-solving process; encourages them to develop critical thinking and problem-solving skills.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td style="width: 15%; background: #F2F2F2; color: #000000; font-size: 13px; line-height: 1.3; text-align: left; vertical-align: top; padding: 4px 3px; border: 1px solid #17365D;"><strong>Purdue</strong></td>
<td style="width: 19%; background: #F2F2F2; color: #000000; font-size: 13px; line-height: 1.3; text-align: left; vertical-align: top; padding: 4px 3px; border: 1px solid #17365D;">Every graduate will need to have AI competency; each college and department determines what that means; each year to have <a href="https://engineering.purdue.edu/ai_initiatives/engineering-education">progressively more AI integration</a>; students must get the fundamentals right, per industry stakeholders.</td>
<td style="width: 20%; background: #F2F2F2; color: #000000; font-size: 13px; line-height: 1.3; text-align: left; vertical-align: top; padding: 4px 3px; border: 1px solid #17365D;">Physics, math; fundamentals of AI technology; statistical thinking, probabilistic reasoning to make sense of data; algorithmic reasoning; programming; data analysis; Python for data science class to look at data and programming together; verification.</td>
<td style="width: 22%; background: #F2F2F2; color: #000000; font-size: 13px; line-height: 1.3; text-align: left; vertical-align: top; padding: 4px 3px; border: 1px solid #17365D;">Human perspectives, creativity, originality, and ability to make judgment; understanding how AI works and what it can and can&#8217;t do; utilizing AI in projects; how to be a manager of AI; building intuition and knowledge to keep moving forward without AI doing everything for them.</td>
<td style="width: 24%; background: #F2F2F2; color: #000000; font-size: 13px; line-height: 1.3; text-align: left; vertical-align: top; padding: 4px 3px; border: 1px solid #17365D;">Launched <a style="color: #0563c1; text-decoration: underline;" href="https://engineering.purdue.edu/Engr/AboutUs/News/Spotlights/2026/2026-0415-Purdue-Engineering-launches-free-AI-bootcamp-to-build-job-ready-competency-ahead-of-university-wide-requirement" target="_blank" rel="noopener">summer bootcamp</a> for graduating class, including how to do an interview in the AI era according to recent grads; how to prepare a portfolio or resume; <a style="color: #0563c1; text-decoration: underline;" href="https://engineering.purdue.edu/Engr/Academics/Graduate/combined-degree-programs" target="_blank" rel="noopener">4+1 program</a> for extra training; <a style="color: #0563c1; text-decoration: underline;" href="https://engineering.purdue.edu/semiconductors" target="_blank" rel="noopener">semiconductor degrees</a> program, advised by a 40- member semiconductor degrees leadership board; semiconductor career fair.</td>

		<item>
		<title>The Complexity Between The Bits</title>
		<link>https://semiengineering.com/the-complexity-between-the-bits/</link>
					<comments>https://semiengineering.com/the-complexity-between-the-bits/#respond</comments>
		
		<dc:creator><![CDATA[Vidya Chandran Darbari]]></dc:creator>
		<pubDate>Thu, 29 Oct 2026 07:09:52 +0000</pubDate>
				<category><![CDATA[Blogs - SLD]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Making Formal Normal]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[System-Level Design]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Axiomise]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[clock gating]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[formal]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[formal verification]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[metastability]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[verification]]></category>
		<guid isPermaLink="false">https://semiengineering.com/?p=24283420</guid>

					<description><![CDATA[<p>What biological reasoning can teach us about verifying silicon systems.</p>
<p>The post <a href="https://semiengineering.com/the-complexity-between-the-bits/">The Complexity Between The Bits</a> appeared first on <a href="https://semiengineering.com">Semiconductor Engineering</a>.</p>
]]></description>
										<content:encoded><![CDATA[<p><strong>By Vidya Chandran Darbari and Ashish Darbari</strong></p>
<p>A recent conversation about carbon intelligence and silicon intelligence stayed with me. What interested me was not which one wins, but the complexity we take on as we ask silicon to sense, interpret, decide and act.</p>
<p>My work as an experimental structural biologist taught me to ask what a structure could explain, and what it could not. Solving a molecular structure was not the same as explaining the behaviour of the system to which it belonged. The next question was always about mechanism: what happens when the parts interact? That question shaped my perspective as a deep generalist in life sciences, connecting molecular mechanisms, biological networks and system behaviour.</p>
<p>Now, as Chief Strategy and Operations Officer at Axiomise, I return to that question in a different setting.</p>
<p>A chip is not a cell. But investigating biological complexity can sharpen how we investigate engineered systems—provided the comparison changes a question we ask, an observation we make or an obligation we verify. Otherwise, it remains an interesting analogy.</p>
<h2>A map is not a mechanism</h2>
<p>In life sciences, a protein interaction map is where questions begin. A connection identifies a possible interaction; it does not establish when that interaction matters. Concentration, localization, binding affinity, feedback and timing influence the outcome.</p>
<p>The same messenger can stimulate or suppress an immune response depending on the receptor and the receiving cell&#8217;s state. The molecule has not become inconsistent. Our description was incomplete without its context.</p>
<p>Silicon engineers already understand dependencies. The useful transfer is not simply to think in networks, but to distinguish connectivity from the conditions under which connected components produce a particular behaviour.</p>
<p>A link can transport packets correctly, a controller can enter its power states correctly, and an interconnect can arbitrate correctly under its assumptions. Their combination can still violate a requirement if those assumptions cease to hold.</p>
<p>The engineering consequence is to make context explicit: which neighbouring states, available resources and event orderings does each guarantee depend on? Those conditions belong in verification, not just in integration notes.</p>
<h2>Timing is part of the meaning</h2>
<p>Our senses make context tangible. Combining cues helps only when their timing supports treating them as evidence of the same event. More signals do not automatically resolve ambiguity.</p>
<p>Consider an illustrative edge controller combining camera and distance-sensor data. Both measurements could be accurately encoded, transported without corruption and individually plausible. If they describe different moments but are treated as simultaneous, the fused estimate can represent a situation that never existed.</p>
<p>The bits arrive intact while the interpretation is wrong.</p>
<p>The biological perspective directs attention to the signal&#8217;s relationship with the state in which it is received. The engineering response is concrete: timestamp association, freshness, allowable skew and missing observations belong in the requirements.</p>
<p>Similar questions arise during reset, link retraining and power transitions. What happens to a transaction accepted before the transition? Which state survives recovery? When can a receiver safely treat new data as valid?</p>
<p>An interface is a relationship over time, not merely a set of wires. Verification must capture that relationship, particularly when the resulting interpretation controls an action.</p>
<h2>Different uncertainties need different evidence</h2>
<p>It is tempting to describe biology as grey and silicon as black and white. That contrast obscures more than it explains.</p>
<p>Biological models can combine continuous quantities, discrete events, deterministic equations and stochastic processes. These are separate distinctions. A graded response need not be random.</p>
<p>A digital block can have a uniquely defined next state for a given state and input while leaving a system question unresolved: which input sequences and environmental behaviours must we consider? Formal verification can examine behaviours admitted by a model under stated assumptions. That is not physical randomness.</p>
<p>Beneath the abstraction sits physics. Metastability is not digital logic becoming analog because an architecture grew complicated. It is physical behaviour the design must accommodate.</p>
<p>The differences matter because a physical measurement, statistical association and logical proof answer different questions. Learning from biology should make us more precise about where uncertainty resides—not less demanding about correctness.</p>
<h2>Investigate what would distinguish the explanations</h2>
<p>My life-sciences training made me wary of assuming that a symptom identifies its cause.</p>
<p>Consider an illustrative chiplet system, not a customer incident. A transaction times out. A power controller has gated a clock before outstanding traffic drained. It will restore the clock after receiving a wake request, but that request is queued behind the stalled traffic.</p>
<p>Each side waits for the other. The timeout is the symptom; the mechanism is a circular dependency. Extending the timeout would not remove it.</p>
<p>Other mechanisms could produce the same symptom: a lost credit, inconsistent reset state or starvation. Reproducing a trace does not distinguish those explanations.</p>
<p>Here, experimental reasoning suggests a useful question: which intervention would separate the hypotheses? What happens if the clock remains running? What if wake delivery is independent of normal traffic? Does the failure persist when downstream service changes?</p>
<p>These are familiar verification activities. Their explanatory purpose matters: choose an investigation because its outcome challenges a mechanism, not merely because it produces another passing run.</p>
<p>A counterexample also needs interpretation. It may expose a defect, an unrealistic environment or behaviour introduced by abstraction. A passing result needs equal scrutiny: did the assumptions exclude the situation we needed to examine?</p>
<h2>Connect evidence across layers</h2>
<p>Life sciences investigates activity at different levels: genome sequence, gene expression, protein machinery and metabolism. These measurements are not interchangeable. Discrepancies can reveal regulation, compensation or an incomplete explanation.</p>
<p>There is no exact mapping from these layers to a chip. The transferable principle is to connect evidence across levels rather than expect one measurement to explain everything.</p>
<p>RTL and configuration describe one view. Workload activity, transaction traces and physical telemetry provide others. Imagine a workload increasing power demand, a thermal policy reducing frequency and a controller approaching its deadline. Neither the transaction trace nor the temperature reading alone establishes whether the complete sequence was permitted.</p>
<p>I see an opportunity for tools that organize observations around shared events and competing explanations. AI-assisted analysis could help propose relationships, but correlation should lead to a testable hypothesis, not be labeled a root cause.</p>
<p>Observability should follow the same reasoning. For the clock-gating example, we need to relate outstanding traffic, clock decisions, wake generation and wake delivery. More logs are not necessarily better evidence if those relationships remain invisible.</p>
<h2>Turn mechanisms into obligations</h2>
<p>Once we understand a mechanism, we should not leave it in a debug report.</p>
<p>What must prevent the circular dependency? Must traffic drain before clock gating? Must wake delivery remain independent? What happens if the normal sequence is interrupted?</p>
<p>These questions turn explanation into architectural obligations. This is the connection I see between biological reasoning and formal-first engineering: one directs attention towards context and competing mechanisms; the other helps express and check precise requirements in a design model.</p>
<p>At Axiomise, formal-first means asking those questions while the architecture can still change. Credits must not be lost or double-counted. Ordering must be preserved where required. Reset and recovery must restore compatible endpoint states.</p>
<p>Progress needs its own argument. Nothing being corrupted does not establish that an accepted request eventually completes. Service and fairness assumptions must be justified by the architecture.</p>
<p>Local proofs need the same treatment. If one component assumes a neighbour responds, where is that response established? Individually successful checks cannot establish a system claim when their assumptions are incompatible or circular.</p>
<p>Formal-first is not formal-only. Simulation, emulation, physical analysis and system validation contribute different evidence. The task is to connect them around identifiable questions.</p>
<h2>Ownership should follow the obligation</h2>
<p>If a failure mechanism crosses power management, traffic control and recovery, preventing it cannot depend solely on each team completing local checks.</p>
<p>Someone must own the cross-system obligation: what must remain true, which components contribute, which assumptions are required and which changes trigger reassessment. A revised power policy should reopen the progress argument if it changes its conditions.</p>
<p>This does not diminish specialist expertise. It gives the relationships between specialisms the same standing as the components they produce.</p>
<p>Other fields will not hand silicon engineering a ready-made solution. They can help us recognize incomplete descriptions and design better investigations. Biology&#8217;s contribution is not permission to accept ambiguity, but a discipline for challenging explanations.</p>
<p>Looking back at structural biology, I recognize the temptation to let a beautifully resolved model feel like the end of the investigation. The important question was what it helped explain—and what should follow.</p>
<p>The bits can be binary. Our understanding of the system is not automatically complete.</p>
<p><em>Ashish Darbari is the founder and CEO of Axiomise.</em></p>
<p>The post <a href="https://semiengineering.com/the-complexity-between-the-bits/">The Complexity Between The Bits</a> appeared first on <a href="https://semiengineering.com">Semiconductor Engineering</a>.</p>
]]></content:encoded>

		<item>
		<title>Is It Getting Hot In Here? Why Thermal Is No Longer A Signoff Problem</title>
		<link>https://semiengineering.com/is-it-getting-hot-in-here-why-thermal-is-no-longer-a-signoff-problem/</link>
					<comments>https://semiengineering.com/is-it-getting-hot-in-here-why-thermal-is-no-longer-a-signoff-problem/#respond</comments>
		
		<dc:creator><![CDATA[Chris Mueth]]></dc:creator>
		<pubDate>Thu, 29 Oct 2026 07:08:48 +0000</pubDate>
				<category><![CDATA[Blogs - SLD]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[First By Design]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[System-Level Design]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Keysight]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[multiphysics]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[shift left]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[thermal]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[thermal analysis]]></category>
		<guid isPermaLink="false">https://semiengineering.com/?p=24283449</guid>

					<description><![CDATA[<p>Applying the appropriate level of simulation and abstraction at each stage of the design process is key to effective thermal analysis.</p>
<p>The post <a href="https://semiengineering.com/is-it-getting-hot-in-here-why-thermal-is-no-longer-a-signoff-problem/">Is It Getting Hot In Here? Why Thermal Is No Longer A Signoff Problem</a> appeared first on <a href="https://semiengineering.com">Semiconductor Engineering</a>.</p>
]]></description>
										<content:encoded><![CDATA[<p style="line-height: 150%;">AI, high-performance computing and advanced semiconductor packaging have pushed power density higher, and that means managing heat is becoming one of the most consequential challenges in electronic design.</p>
<p style="line-height: 150%;">The issue is no longer simply how to cool a chip after it has been designed. Thermal behavior now affects decisions spanning device performance, package architecture, mechanical stress, reliability, floor planning and ultimately system performance. So engineering teams must shift thermal analysis to earlier in the design process as they consider a much broader multi-physics strategy.</p>
<p style="line-height: 150%;">Traditionally, engineers could isolate and analyze many physical effects separately. Electrical performance could be evaluated in one environment, thermal behavior in another and mechanical stress in another and so on. But with increasing integration, a change intended to improve performance in one domain can create unintended consequences elsewhere. Higher power can increase temperature; temperature can change electrical behavior; and thermal expansion can introduce mechanical stress that ultimately affects reliability.</p>
<p style="line-height: 150%;">This is particularly worrisome when it comes to chiplets, 3D-ICs and other advanced packaging architectures. Multiple dies, interconnects, substrates, materials and cooling approaches interact within a tightly integrated structure. Designers need to understand not only what happens within an individual die but how the entire assembly behaves as a system.</p>
<p style="line-height: 150%;">And addressing this means examining both accuracy and scale. The engineer developing a chip may need detailed information about localized behavior and potential hotspots. A package engineer needs a different level of abstraction, while a system architect may need to evaluate thermal and performance tradeoffs across an even larger environment. Simulating every element at maximum fidelity throughout the entire system would be computationally impractical.</p>
<p style="line-height: 150%;">The answer is not simply more simulation. It is applying the appropriate level of simulation and abstraction at each stage of the design process. Techniques such as reduced-order modeling and superposition can help engineering teams scale analysis while preserving the information needed to make architectural decisions. These techniques provide each engineer with the fidelity required for the task while maintaining connections between the physical domains that influence one another.</p>
<p style="line-height: 150%;">This also changes when multi-physics analysis needs to occur. Waiting until final verification to discover that a floor plan creates an unacceptable thermal or mechanical condition can lead to expensive redesigns or even a complete (and expensive) design re-spin. If engineers bring multi-physics analysis into architectural exploration and floor planning, they can evaluate performance, package size, thermal behavior and reliability together, when there is still flexibility to make meaningful changes.</p>
<p style="line-height: 150%;">AI and agentic engineering workflows offer another opportunity to accelerate this process by using Model Context Protocol (MCP) to help orchestrate increasingly complex tools, models and engineering data. But AI does not eliminate the need for trusted physics-based simulation. AI-generated decisions can be difficult to trace, and models can make mistakes. Engineering teams therefore need workflows that combine automation with reliable simulation and sufficient visibility into how results are produced.</p>
<p style="line-height: 150%;">This growing thermal challenge is part of a larger transformation in electronic design. Electrical, thermal, mechanical and other physical effects can no longer be treated as isolated disciplines. They are interconnected elements of the same engineering problem.</p>
<p style="line-height: 150%;">Keysight’s goal is to enable engineers to bring these domains together earlier in the development process, applying the right physics, fidelity and automation at the right time. By shifting multi-physics analysis left – from final verification toward architecture and design exploration –engineering teams can identify problems sooner, make better-informed tradeoffs and increase confidence that increasingly complex electronic systems will perform as intended before committing to physical hardware.</p>
<p style="line-height: 150%;">So is it getting hot in here? Absolutely. But with the right approach to design exploration and analysis, it doesn&#8217;t have to be your problem.</p>
<p>The post <a href="https://semiengineering.com/is-it-getting-hot-in-here-why-thermal-is-no-longer-a-signoff-problem/">Is It Getting Hot In Here? Why Thermal Is No Longer A Signoff Problem</a> appeared first on <a href="https://semiengineering.com">Semiconductor Engineering</a>.</p>
]]></content:encoded>

		<post-id xmlns="com-wordpress:feed-additions:1">24283449</post-id>	</item>
		<item>
		<title>Physics-Based Validation: The Foundation Of Trustworthy Agentic EDA</title>
		<link>https://semiengineering.com/physics-based-validation-the-foundation-of-trustworthy-agentic-eda/</link>
					<comments>https://semiengineering.com/physics-based-validation-the-foundation-of-trustworthy-agentic-eda/#respond</comments>
		
		<dc:creator><![CDATA[Emma-Jane Crozier]]></dc:creator>
		<pubDate>Thu, 29 Oct 2026 07:07:05 +0000</pubDate>
				<category><![CDATA[A System Perspective]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Blogs - SLD]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[System-Level Design]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[3D ICs]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[agentic AI]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[AI]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[EDA]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[multiphysics]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[physics-based design]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Siemens EDA]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[validation]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[verification]]></category>
		<guid isPermaLink="false">https://semiengineering.com/?p=24283431</guid>

					<description><![CDATA[<p>What self-verifying agents validate their decisions against and why physics-based engines are the authority on correctness. </p>
<p>The post <a href="https://semiengineering.com/physics-based-validation-the-foundation-of-trustworthy-agentic-eda/">Physics-Based Validation: The Foundation Of Trustworthy Agentic EDA</a> appeared first on <a href="https://semiengineering.com">Semiconductor Engineering</a>.</p>
]]></description>
										<content:encoded><![CDATA[<p>This is the third article in a series examining what it takes to build production-ready AI agents for EDA. The first explored the architectural decisions required for agents to operate reliably in production environments. The second examined what self-verifying means in an agentic EDA workflow: how agents continuously check their decisions as they work, rather than waiting until the end of a run.</p>
<p>But what exactly are those decisions being verified against?</p>
<p>The answer is physics. More specifically, the deterministic, physics-based EDA engines that define whether a design is correct. A self-verifying agent checks its own work, and a physics-validated workflow confirms that work is correct.</p>
<h2>Semiconductor design is governed by physics</h2>
<p>Chip design has always faced physics-based constraints. Every transistor, every wire, every timing path, and every thermal gradient is governed by the fixed laws of physics, regardless of how the design was created. This is why tapeout is binary: silicon either works or it does not.</p>
<p>When AI enters this domain, that physical reality does not change. The relevant question is not whether an AI system can reason about physics, but whether the outputs it produces have been confirmed by the engines that enforce that physics.</p>
<p>That distinction separates a model that is capable of reasoning about physics from a workflow that is physics-validated.</p>
<h2>What physics-validated means</h2>
<p>A physics-validated workflow is one in which every agent decision is confirmed against a deterministic, physics-based EDA engine before the next step begins. Validation does not happen only at the end of a workflow or after an entire run is complete. It happens continuously, at every step, against the engines engineers have trusted for decades.</p>
<p>The agent executes a step, and the engine determines whether the result meets the design&#8217;s physical constraints. If it does not, the agent identifies the issue, resolves it, and continues. Physics-validated means the engine, not the model, is the authority on correctness.</p>
<h2>Why it matters at tapeout</h2>
<p>In semiconductor design, there is zero margin for unverified decisions. Tapeout dates are fixed, respins are expensive, and missed market windows do not come back.</p>
<p>For engineering organizations deploying agentic AI at scale, the key question is not simply whether the workflow is fast. It is whether the outcomes it produces can be trusted without requiring engineers to manually re-verify every autonomous decision.</p>
<p>An agentic workflow that reasons correctly about physics but does not validate its outputs against physics-based engines introduces risk at every autonomous step. The longer a workflow runs without validation, the further a design can drift from correctness before an error surfaces.</p>
<p>In a long-running autonomous workflow that executes across multiple tools, physical domains, and hours without human intervention, unvalidated decisions can compound. Continuous physics-based validation prevents those errors from propagating through the workflow.</p>
<h2>3D-IC design: A multi-physics challenge</h2>
<p>In Part 2, I described an autonomous library characterization workflow in which the agent continuously checks its work against physics-based EDA engines. That workflow demonstrates the principle at a specific task level. The need for continuous validation increases when a workflow spans multiple tools and physical domains, and 3D-IC design shows why. It is the ultimate test of physics, and the clearest example of why semiconductor design is physics.</p>
<p>AI factories require more compute, memory bandwidth, and thermal performance within an increasingly constrained physical footprint. As power, performance, and area scaling on monolithic dies reaches practical limits, chiplet-based 2.5D and 3D architectures allow compute and memory to scale through tightly integrated packages.</p>
<p>That level of integration makes the physical design problem considerably more complex. Stacked active dies make heat removal and temperature interactions harder to predict, while packaging can introduce mechanical stress. Electrical, power, thermal, and mechanical effects are interconnected, so a change in one area can affect the others. Addressing these effects through manual, fragmented, and tool-intensive workflows creates frequent engineering handoffs, slows iteration, and limits the number of design alternatives teams can evaluate.</p>
<h2>An autonomous workflow for 3D-IC design</h2>
<p>Siemens has developed an agentic engineering workflow that autonomously coordinates 3D-IC design and analysis. MCP servers and skill files for different EDA tools are connected to Fuse EDA AI Agent, which acts as an MCP aggregator that can be connected to any coding agent of choice. In this particular example, Claude Code is used as the agentic coding IDE, orchestrating a nine-stage electro-thermal workflow spanning seven Siemens EDA tools, running 100 to 200 agentic turns over more than 12 hours.</p>
<p>Each stage performs a distinct engineering task, from floor planning, library characterization, and power analysis to thermal signoff, system-level thermal simulation, parasitic extraction, and power- and temperature-aware SPICE simulation. Every stage is validated using signoff-grade, physics-based solvers.</p>
<p>The workflow runs autonomously, executing analyses, evaluating results, identifying missed targets, and continuing to refine the design. Teams can sweep heat transfer coefficient (HTC) boundaries, power budgets, and material stack-ups across 200 to 500+ design points per cycle. This enables teams to configure tools up to 20 times faster, reduce engineering effort by up to 95%, and evaluate up to 50 times more design options than a conventional manual flow.</p>
<p>The demo below shows the electro-thermal workflow in action:</p>
<p><iframe loading="lazy" src="https://www.youtube.com/embed/6bp1FByiKXU" width="480" height="270" frameborder="0" allowfullscreen="allowfullscreen"></iframe></p>
<h2>How do I know if my workflow is physics-validated?</h2>
<p>When evaluating an agentic EDA workflow, one question determines whether its architecture is physics-validated: At each step, is the agent&#8217;s output confirmed by a deterministic, physics-based EDA engine before the workflow proceeds?</p>
<p>If the answer is yes, the workflow is physics-validated. The engine is the arbiter, and every autonomous decision is confirmed against the physical reality of the design.</p>
<h2>Defining trusted autonomy in EDA</h2>
<p>Taken together, the three articles in this series define the requirements for production-ready agentic EDA: an architecture designed for real engineering environments, a self-verifying mechanism that continuously checks agent decisions, and a physics-validated standard that determines whether those decisions are correct.</p>
<p>This is the architectural standard Siemens EDA has built into the <a href="https://www.siemens.com/en-us/products/fuse-eda-ai-system/agent/">Fuse EDA AI Agent</a>. The result is trusted autonomy: validated engineering outcomes at every step of the workflow.</p>
<p><strong>Read the first two articles in the series:</strong></p>
<ul>
<li>Part 1: <a href="https://semiengineering.com/the-architecture-decisions-behind-a-production-ready-eda-ai-agent/">The Architecture Decisions Behind a Production-Ready EDA AI Agent</a></li>
<li>Part 2: <a href="https://semiengineering.com/what-self-verifying-means-in-agentic-eda-workflows-and-why-it-matters/">What Self-Verifying Means in Agentic EDA Workflows and Why It Matters</a></li>
</ul>
<p>The post <a href="https://semiengineering.com/physics-based-validation-the-foundation-of-trustworthy-agentic-eda/">Physics-Based Validation: The Foundation Of Trustworthy Agentic EDA</a> appeared first on <a href="https://semiengineering.com">Semiconductor Engineering</a>.</p>

					<description><![CDATA[<p>Determining which products may fall within the CRA's scope and what information may need to be provided to customers.</p>
<p>The post <a href="https://semiengineering.com/the-eu-cyber-resilience-act-reaches-chip-and-ip-suppliers/">The EU Cyber Resilience Act Reaches Chip And IP Suppliers</a> appeared first on <a href="https://semiengineering.com">Semiconductor Engineering</a>.</p>
]]></description>
										<content:encoded><![CDATA[<p>Many of today’s electronic products connect to networks and other devices to communicate, exchange data, coordinate tasks, and receive software updates. These connections make everyday work-life tasks incredibly convenient. However, this convenience also comes with risks. Weaknesses in these products can give cyberattacks a way into trusted devices. Responding to an attack takes time and money. Even worse, reputational damage can last long after the incident. To help reduce these risks, the European Union (EU) established common cybersecurity requirements that set a baseline for product development.</p>
<h2>The 2024 framework</h2>
<p>The Cyber Resilience Act (CRA), adopted in October 2024, establishes the requirements framework. Companies supplying digital products to the EU market must consider the CRA, whether they sell directly or supply components for a customer’s finished product. The rules apply regardless of where a company is based or where development takes place.</p>
<p><img data-recalc-dims="1" loading="lazy" decoding="async" class="alignnone size-full wp-image-24283425" src="https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/10/Arteris_EU-CRA-Reaches-Chip-IP-Suppliers-fig0.webp?resize=1020%2C515&#038;ssl=1" alt="" width="1020" height="515" srcset="https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/10/Arteris_EU-CRA-Reaches-Chip-IP-Suppliers-fig0.webp?w=1020&amp;ssl=1 1020w, https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/10/Arteris_EU-CRA-Reaches-Chip-IP-Suppliers-fig0.webp?resize=300%2C151&amp;ssl=1 300w, https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/10/Arteris_EU-CRA-Reaches-Chip-IP-Suppliers-fig0.webp?resize=768%2C388&amp;ssl=1 768w, https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/10/Arteris_EU-CRA-Reaches-Chip-IP-Suppliers-fig0.webp?resize=600%2C303&amp;ssl=1 600w" sizes="auto, (max-width: 1000px) 100vw, 1000px" /></p>
<p>The regulation applies to semiconductors sold separately. Manufacturers must exercise due diligence when incorporating third-party components into their products. Semiconductor intellectual property (IP) suppliers may need to provide security evaluation results and explain the assumptions for secure use.</p>
<h2>The 2026 requirements</h2>
<p>The CRA addresses widespread product vulnerabilities, inconsistent security updates, and insufficient information for secure use. The CRA&#8217;s mandatory reporting phase took effect in September 2026. When manufacturers learn that a vulnerability in their product is being actively exploited, or that a severe incident has affected its security, an early warning must be submitted. A more detailed notification follows, with a final report explaining the findings and corrective measures. Manufacturers submit these notifications through the CRA’s Single Reporting Platform.</p>
<p>The CRA also lists semiconductor products such as microprocessors, microcontrollers, ASICs, and FPGAs according to core functionality, including security-related functions. The applicable category and obligations depend on the product and how it is placed on the market. Chip and IP companies need to understand which CRA responsibilities apply to their products and what information they may need to provide to customers. That determination can be challenging because commitments vary with the product’s classification. Figure 1 illustrates how suppliers may provide security evidence to support CRA reporting.</p>
<p><img data-recalc-dims="1" loading="lazy" decoding="async" class="alignnone size-full wp-image-24283426" src="https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/10/Arteris_EU-CRA-Reaches-Chip-IP-Suppliers-fig1.webp?resize=653%2C314&#038;ssl=1" alt="" width="653" height="314" srcset="https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/10/Arteris_EU-CRA-Reaches-Chip-IP-Suppliers-fig1.webp?w=653&amp;ssl=1 653w, https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/10/Arteris_EU-CRA-Reaches-Chip-IP-Suppliers-fig1.webp?resize=300%2C144&amp;ssl=1 300w, https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/10/Arteris_EU-CRA-Reaches-Chip-IP-Suppliers-fig1.webp?resize=600%2C289&amp;ssl=1 600w" sizes="auto, (max-width: 653px) 100vw, 653px" /></p>
<p><strong>Fig. 1: Illustrative supply chain showing how component information and security evidence may move between suppliers and the product manufacturer in support of its EU CRA documentation. (Source: Arteris, Inc.)</strong></p>
<h2>CRA readiness</h2>
<p>To help companies prepare, <a href="https://bit.ly/3KcLBOH">Arteris</a> developed the <a href="https://explore.arteris.com/eu-cra-hardware-readiness-guide?_gl=1*3dmh5d*_gcl_au*MTU1ODQ5NDA2MC4xNzkyNDQ3MTMy*_ga*MTM1MzU0MDY5Mi4xNzA4NTM2NzQy*_ga_1JF4K2M1K9*czE3OTI1NDI5MDEkbzM5NyRnMSR0MTc5MjU0MzA4OSRqNDUkbDAkaDA.">EU CRA Hardware Readiness Guide</a>. It provides a checklist to help companies assess which products may fall within the CRA’s scope, identify who is responsible for each product, and determine what information customers may request. It also helps identify where classification, responsibility, processes, or security documentation may need further work. The guide distinguishes design-stage security evidence from a completed conformity assessment. The results can support a manufacturer’s compliance work, but it does not cover all of the manufacturer’s CRA duties.</p>
<p>For the security assessment, the checklist asks companies to identify assets such as keys, configuration, and debug state. Companies can also define protection boundaries and document where those assets can and cannot travel. A design that performs its specified functions does not, by itself, show that sensitive assets remain protected.</p>
<p>Suppliers can use the readiness guide to organize information and security evidence that may support customers as they prepare their technical documentation. Annex VII of the CRA lists what manufacturers must provide in their technical documentation. It includes cybersecurity risk assessments, relevant design and development information, and supporting evidence, where applicable. Dated, repeatable evaluations can help document the design examined, describe what was covered, and note any limitations. The guide also recommends mapping evaluation findings to named hardware weakness classes. The CRA sets security outcomes but does not mandate a specific evaluation method.</p>
<p>The Arteris checklist helps companies organize their preparation. In addition, <a href="https://hubs.ly/Q04gDnWv0">Cycuity</a>, an Arteris brand, offers <a href="https://hubs.ly/Q04gDxrF0">Radix</a> for hardware security verification, helping developers generate traceable security evidence. It translates security requirements into rules that can be checked in existing simulation and emulation flows. <a href="https://hubs.ly/Q04gDDnP0">Radix-S</a> checks those rules during simulation, and <a href="https://hubs.ly/Q04gDDnP0">Radix-M</a> carries them into emulation, allowing system-level analysis.</p>
<p>The software tracks designated assets, such as cryptographic keys and configuration data, through a design to identify flows that violate defined rules. It can also help assess whether Root of Trust features work as intended. Findings help companies locate potential weaknesses, measure security coverage, and identify areas for further testing.</p>
<h2>What changes in 2027</h2>
<p>December 11, 2027, marks the next major deadline. The CRA’s broader cybersecurity, conformity assessment, and documentation requirements take effect. CE marking will also be required, which declares that the product conforms to applicable EU requirements.</p>
<p>Before the full requirements take effect, hardware and IP suppliers can work with customers to identify which product versions are intended for the EU market. Early identification gives both sides time to align program schedules and plan necessary work while designs are still in development.</p>
<p><a href="https://bit.ly/3KcLBOH">Arteris</a> can help companies make sense of the CRA’s requirements and consider how they apply to hardware and IP products. The <a href="https://explore.arteris.com/eu-cra-hardware-readiness-guide?_gl=1*3dmh5d*_gcl_au*MTU1ODQ5NDA2MC4xNzkyNDQ3MTMy*_ga*MTM1MzU0MDY5Mi4xNzA4NTM2NzQy*_ga_1JF4K2M1K9*czE3OTI1NDI5MDEkbzM5NyRnMSR0MTc5MjU0MzA4OSRqNDUkbDAkaDA.">EU CRA Hardware Readiness Guide</a> offers a practical starting point, and Arteris teams can help companies work through questions as they prepare.</p>
<p>The post <a href="https://semiengineering.com/the-eu-cyber-resilience-act-reaches-chip-and-ip-suppliers/">The EU Cyber Resilience Act Reaches Chip And IP Suppliers</a> appeared first on <a href="https://semiengineering.com">Semiconductor Engineering</a>.</p>
]]></content:encoded>

		<title>From Functional Monitoring To In-System Test: Extending Voltage-Aware Observability</title>
		<link>https://semiengineering.com/from-functional-monitoring-to-in-system-test-extending-voltage-aware-observability/</link>
					<comments>https://semiengineering.com/from-functional-monitoring-to-in-system-test-extending-voltage-aware-observability/#respond</comments>
		
		<dc:creator><![CDATA[Aakash Jani]]></dc:creator>
		<pubDate>Thu, 29 Oct 2026 07:05:54 +0000</pubDate>
				<category><![CDATA[Blogs - SLD]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Clock Talk]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[System-Level Design]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[in-system test]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Movellus]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[on-chip monitors]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Siemens EDA]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[silicon lifecycle management]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[SLM]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[structural test]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[test]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[voltage droop]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[voltage droop sensors]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[voltage observability]]></category>
		<guid isPermaLink="false">https://semiengineering.com/?p=24283015</guid>

					<description><![CDATA[<p>Separate false failures caused by voltage droop from real structural defects.</p>
<p>The post <a href="https://semiengineering.com/from-functional-monitoring-to-in-system-test-extending-voltage-aware-observability/">From Functional Monitoring To In-System Test: Extending Voltage-Aware Observability</a> appeared first on <a href="https://semiengineering.com">Semiconductor Engineering</a>.</p>
]]></description>
										<content:encoded><![CDATA[<p><strong>By Aakash Jani, Henrique Mendes, and Robert Wilcox</strong></p>
<p><a href="https://semiengineering.com/detect-diagnose-and-debug-using-sensors-and-functional-monitoring/">In a previous blog</a>, we highlighted the importance of combining physical and functional sensors to plan for new workloads. This use case remains relevant for high-performance AI and HPC systems because ‘spiky’ workloads induce severe droops that dominate power budgets.</p>
<p>Your $1 billion ASIC just had a scan failure during an In-System Test. How can you tell whether the failure is an actual structural failure, or one caused by physical phenomena like voltage droop? Scan patterns can often generate intense switching activity when long or multiple chains are toggled, which may induce a voltage droop. If the failure is due to a voltage droop, you have a few options: slow the shift clock, raise the voltage, or repartition your scan patterns. Without physical sensors that report conditions during the test, teams can’t tell the two cases apart and might throw away good parts on false failures.</p>
<p>Co-mingling physical and functional sensors is necessary across the system lifecycle because functional and physical failures are connected. Let’s move further down the system lifecycle into silicon deployment for another example.</p>
<p>The previous demo combined the Movellus Aeonic Insight Droop Detector with Siemens Tessent Embedded Analytics to correlate voltage events with processor and interconnect activity. Today, we are proud to announce an extension of physical and functional monitoring for system lifecycle management: adding Tessent In-System Test (IST) to the demo. Running structural test on deployed systems means the false-failure question follows the part off the tester and into the field, where a wrong call can get a healthy node pulled from service. Combined with voltage monitoring, IST helps engineers separate false failures caused by droop from real structural defects.</p>
<h2>Why add in-system test?</h2>
<p>Meta ran silent-error tests across hundreds of thousands of fleet machines and flagged hundreds of CPUs that corrupted data without reporting an error.<sup>[1]</sup> Google has reported a similar rate in its own fleet.<sup>[2]</sup> Amazon also presented its results from using In-System Test technology on AWS silicon.<sup>[3]</sup> Tessent IST incorporates selected structural tests into deployed systems, allowing testing without removing the device, so faults like these can be investigated early.</p>
<p>However, structural testing can cause intense switching activity, which can lead to voltage droops during tests. These events depend on test patterns, sensor locations, and device conditions. A pass/fail result alone lacks context.</p>
<p>Combining IST with telemetry enables more detailed analysis: Did the droop coincide with testing? Which core was tested? Was other traffic active?</p>
<h2>Demonstrating physical and functional sensors across the system lifecycle</h2>
<p>The updated demonstration integrates Tessent IST, alongside the previous solution, on a Siemens Veloce proFPGA platform: Movellus Aeonic Insight voltage-droop sensors, Tessent Embedded Analytics functional monitors and message infrastructure, and Siemens scan-and-test infrastructure for IST. The target design contains multiple DFT-instrumented RISC-V cores, which drive the workload observed in the functional mode, and then receive scan patterns when brought into test mode.</p>
<p><img data-recalc-dims="1" loading="lazy" decoding="async" class="alignnone wp-image-24283017 size-full" src="https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/10/Movellus_In-System-Test-Voltage-Aware-Observability-fig1.webp?resize=762%2C693&#038;ssl=1" alt="" width="762" height="693" srcset="https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/10/Movellus_In-System-Test-Voltage-Aware-Observability-fig1.webp?w=762&amp;ssl=1 762w, https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/10/Movellus_In-System-Test-Voltage-Aware-Observability-fig1.webp?resize=300%2C273&amp;ssl=1 300w, https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/10/Movellus_In-System-Test-Voltage-Aware-Observability-fig1.webp?resize=600%2C546&amp;ssl=1 600w" sizes="auto, (max-width: 762px) 100vw, 762px" /></p>
<p><strong>Fig. 1: Demo system including Tessent Embedded Analytics, the Movellus Aeonic Insight voltage-droop sensor, and scan-and-test infrastructure.</strong></p>
<p><strong>Functional Mode:</strong> a Movellus voltage droop detector is configured through an APB path and can issue a hardware event when a programmed threshold is crossed. Tessent Embedded Analytics receives the event through its trigger and message infrastructure. A Status Monitor records sensor and system signals with timestamps, while a Bus Monitor reports transaction activity on the SoC interconnect. The workflow shows how a physical event initiates collection of the functional evidence needed to investigate its root cause.</p>
<p><img data-recalc-dims="1" loading="lazy" decoding="async" class="alignnone size-full wp-image-24283018" src="https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/10/Movellus_In-System-Test-Voltage-Aware-Observability-fig2.webp?resize=762%2C545&#038;ssl=1" alt="" width="762" height="545" srcset="https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/10/Movellus_In-System-Test-Voltage-Aware-Observability-fig2.webp?w=762&amp;ssl=1 762w, https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/10/Movellus_In-System-Test-Voltage-Aware-Observability-fig2.webp?resize=300%2C215&amp;ssl=1 300w, https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/10/Movellus_In-System-Test-Voltage-Aware-Observability-fig2.webp?resize=600%2C429&amp;ssl=1 600w" sizes="auto, (max-width: 762px) 100vw, 762px" /></p>
<p><strong>Fig. 2: Functional-mode workflow correlating voltage-droop events with timestamped processor and interconnect activity.</strong></p>
<p><strong>Test Mode:</strong> The Tessent IST support adds test control and test-state visibility. Test patterns are run on the demonstration platform, and test start and stop activity is observed through Tessent Embedded Analytics Status monitoring, which monitors an internal test-mode state, so that the test window can be aligned with voltage data. This gives the demonstration time-aligned test execution and sensor behavior.</p>
<h2>From pass/fail to actionable context</h2>
<p>The value of the combined approach is not that voltage telemetry replaces structural test, or that functional monitors determine whether a test pattern passed. The benefit comes from placing their outputs on a common timeline. Structural test identifies whether the targeted logic responds as expected. The voltage monitor reports the physical conditions during that test. Functional monitors reveal concurrent system activity that may have contributed to those conditions.</p>
<p>For example, if a voltage excursion appears only when a particular pattern group is active, the engineer has a focused starting point for investigation. If the excursion coincides instead with heavy traffic from a neighboring functional core, the test scheduler may avoid running that workload and the test concurrently. Repeated observations across sensor locations can also help determine whether an event is local or shared across part of the power-delivery network. These are diagnostic leads rather than conclusions, but they narrow the search space and reduce dependence on worst-case assumptions.</p>
<p><img data-recalc-dims="1" loading="lazy" decoding="async" class="alignnone size-full wp-image-24283019" src="https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/10/Movellus_In-System-Test-Voltage-Aware-Observability-fig3.webp?resize=762%2C559&#038;ssl=1" alt="" width="762" height="559" srcset="https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/10/Movellus_In-System-Test-Voltage-Aware-Observability-fig3.webp?w=762&amp;ssl=1 762w, https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/10/Movellus_In-System-Test-Voltage-Aware-Observability-fig3.webp?resize=300%2C220&amp;ssl=1 300w, https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/10/Movellus_In-System-Test-Voltage-Aware-Observability-fig3.webp?resize=600%2C440&amp;ssl=1 600w" sizes="auto, (max-width: 762px) 100vw, 762px" /></p>
<p><strong>Fig. 3: Test-mode workflow aligning In-System test execution with voltage-sensor data using Status Monitor.</strong></p>
<h2>One sensor, two operating domains</h2>
<p><a href="https://semiengineering.com/detect-diagnose-and-debug-using-sensors-and-functional-monitoring/">The previous blog</a> showed how a voltage droop detector and functional monitoring can bridge the physical and functional domains. Adding Tessent IST extends that observability into test operation. The same sensor can contribute evidence during normal workloads and while structural test content is being applied, while Tessent Embedded Analytics supplies the timestamped context needed to relate those observations.</p>
<p>The current FPGA demonstration establishes the end-to-end concept by running IST patterns and correlating test start and stop with voltage and functional-monitor data. The next step is to complete direct sensor access through iJTAG, providing a path in which test control, sensor observability and functional telemetry can be coordinated through the deployed system.</p>
<p>Together, these capabilities move in-system diagnosis beyond isolated measurements and toward a repeatable detect-diagnose-test workflow.</p>
<h3>References</h3>
<ol>
<li>H. D. Dixit et al., “Silent data corruptions at scale,” 2021, arXiv:2102.11245.</li>
<li>P. H. Hochschild et al., “Cores that don’t count,” in Proc. 18th Workshop on Hot Topics in Operating Systems (HotOS ’21), 2021.</li>
<li>D. Trock, S. Mahadevan, N. Mukherjee, L. Harrison, J. Rajski, and J. Tyszer, “Deterministic In-Fleet Scan Test for a Cloud Computing Platform,” in 2024 IEEE International Test Conference (ITC), 2024, pp. 391–399.</li>
</ol>
<p><em>Henrique Mendes is a product engineer for Tessent Embedded Analytics at Siemens EDA.</em></p>
<p><em>Robert Wilcox is a product engineer for Tessent Embedded Analytics at Siemens EDA.</em></p>

]]></content:encoded>
